Forget "Malaise 2.0," GM says it's still all-in on EVs and autonomy
2026-10-22

Forget "Malaise 2.0," GM says it's still all-in on EVs and autonomy

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
It’s easy to criticize US automakers and their tepid approach to electric vehicles under the current administration and the expiration of the EV tax credit. It’s also easy to look at the rest of the world’s embrace of EVs and wonder if the US is entering a “Malaise 2.0” era. Which is why GM wants the world to know that it is still focused on its end goals of electrification, autonomy, and improving vehicle quality while reducing development time. At this year’s GM Forward event, company executives announced that its hands-off, eyes-off Super Cruise technology will expand across the lineup and be available on nearly every vehicle. This will include the pickup trucks and the crossovers, and not just the Escalade IQ that it was announced for last year. Sterling Anderson, GM’s head of product, said the company is testing this technology to the tune of 1 million miles (1.6 million km) a month in both the virtual and real world. The goal is that not only would the vehicle be able to take you places, but it could go and do things for you. That sounds rather dystopian. But Anderson told Ars in an interview that once you realize what your vehicle is capable of doing (beyond the paradigm of being a car to go places), it opens up a world of convenience that might be hard to envision right now. He’s not talking about an Optimus-style robot that’ll do your laundry, but a tool that you use every day that expands to do more things. GM’s newly announced OnStar AI will play a role. “Imagine you worked late, and you get in your car to go home,” he told us. “Your car might remember that you ordered takeout from a restaurant recently that you frequent and like. The car could prompt you and place a pick-up order for you, which will be ready when you arrive to pick it up.” Trust is the key here, and Anderson reiterated that several times, suggesting that a car might be a more trusted piece of technology than a mobile phone. “People have a relationship with their cars that they don’t have with other things,” he said. “If your phone randomly suggested your favorite restaurant, it might give you pause, but if your car did so within the appropriate context you might be more willing to accept that.” OnStar AI will do more than just learn your habits; it will be able to help you better understand and better use your vehicle, we’re told. For example: a driver hears the car beep but doesn’t know what the alert was for. In this case, they could ask OnStar and it would be able to tell them—for example, a speed limit warning. Being a specialized AI trained specifically on the details of the car, it can be integrated in a way that other models, like Google’s Gemini, cannot. Thanks to new compute on board, announced at last year’s Forward, the car will be able to handle much of that processing locally without requiring a connection to the cloud at all. But a cloud connection could improve the drive or make it safer. For some time now, automakers and suppliers have promised systems that crowdsource data to infer and predict road conditions—if you’re driving through a snowy area, and the cars in front of you have had traction control events, you could receive a warning to be extra careful as you approach. GM also wants to keep customers within its own ecosystem. Imagine a two-car, two-GM OnStar AI household, and you’re late picking up your child from practice. Here, OnStar AI could look and see your spouse is actually closer, swap roles, and re-direct you to the takeout place to pick up dinner. OK, it’s a very specific use case. Trust continues to be the key. Anderson knows that must be earned, and trust is difficult to get back if it’s broken. His approach is to put that customer trust first in what he does. For example, GM’s new infotainment system will have the ability to record from the cameras as a dash cam and store that video. And it can upload that video to GM’s cloud to access anywhere. But it is end-to-end encrypted, meaning that when it hits GM’s servers it’s in a format that GM can’t read. If law enforcement wanted to view the video, GM wouldn’t be able to help because it could not decrypt the video. That is the type of trust that is absolutely needed when dealing with this type of personal data, because there is a convenience to a lot of this. But the opportunity for that data to be misused is also there—it wasn’t too long ago that GM was found to be selling customers’ data, often without their knowledge, to insurance companies. We’ll want to experience GM’s new attitude in practice, of course, but hearing an automotive executive directly address that concern head-on was refreshing. This all leads into the fully autonomous functionality that GM is working on. Ars also spoke with Rashed Haq, VP of Autonomous Vehicles at GM, about the current state of the level 4 technology, and more importantly, when it’ll be safe enough for customers to use. “We look at two key factors, safety and roadcraft,” Haq told me. Safety obviously includes the vehicle’s occupants, but also the general public. And that requires millions of miles of testing in the real world but also digitally. Automakers like GM will never be able to prepare for every scenario, but it will combine training data with an open vocabulary to assess what information is there and make a driving decision based on that. Haq also reiterated the company’s expectation that when the car is driven by the computer, the responsibility falls on GM and the computer if something goes wrong due to a malfunction. The second key here is roadcraft. That means making sure that the vehicle isn’t creating a hazard or problem for other vehicles on the road. You’ve likely seen videos where Waymo vehicles are all crowded on a street blocking traffic: That’s not something GM wants its cars to do. Eventually, all of this leads back to the idea of a car working for you for tasks that aren’t just driving places. Your car could go pick up your dry cleaning, or at least drive there and wait while someone comes out and deposits the clothes inside it. You could ride to school with a child, both sitting in the back seat, reclaiming some time together because the car is doing the mundane task of driving. It’s an interesting idea and will only happen if consumers trust GM and their vehicle enough to do that, something we won’t know until closer to that technology maturing. GM also announced its Gen-4 EV architecture based on LMR battery technology. As we learned last year, the new lithium manganese-rich battery chemistry promises energy densities close to nickel-manganese-cobalt but without that expensive cobalt, making their cost closer to that of lithium iron phosphate (LFP) chemistry cells. A 30 percent cheaper battery should help GM offer a wide variety of EVs, hopefully including some affordable models, like the now-limited-run Bolt. If GM can also deliver affordable autonomy, built on a foundation of trust and safety, it’ll be a unique offering from a US automaker and will put the company in a better position to take on the challenges from global competition.

333 Tor exit nodes hijacked to intercept SSH traffic and retrieve plaintext email
2026-10-22

333 Tor exit nodes hijacked to intercept SSH traffic and retrieve plaintext email

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Unknown hackers hijacked more than 300 Tor exit nodes and reconfigured them to favor unencrypted email traffic and redirect SSH connections through servers they controlled, a researcher reported Tuesday. The hijacking began in late August, when, according to Tor volunteers, the attacker used a valid password that had been reused to gain access to a Tor management server. The attacker then installed Ebury, a backdoor written for Linux that was discovered 12 years ago. Then the attacker connected from the management server to a second Tor server, called Switzerland2, through SSH, using an RSA key that, for unknown reasons, investigators couldn’t find among Switzerland2’s authorized keys. With administrative control of Switzerland2, the attackers installed Ebury there as well. Finally, the attacker reconfigured settings on two exit nodes that Switzerland2 hosted. Ultimately, 333 exit relays were involved in the SSH and email interception campaign, although investigators haven’t established how the attackers gained control of all the underlying servers. The attackers then reconfigured the servers. One set of changes redirected SSH traffic traversing Tor to one of 12 attacker-controlled interceptor servers. Tor users who ignored warnings that the SSH host key had changed would then enter their SSH passwords. A second set of changes caused the exit nodes to block ports 25, 465, 587, 993, and 995—used to accept various forms of encrypted email—while continuing to allow ports for receiving unencrypted email. If email services downgraded the connections, the attackers used their access to the exit nodes to obtain unencrypted messages. It’s unclear whether any such downgrade attempts succeeded. The hijacking occurred from October 2 to October 5. Tor volunteers partially outlined the initial compromise of the management server and Switzerland2 and the attempt to intercept unencrypted email. Tuesday’s report revealed many new details, including how many exit nodes were compromised and how the attackers used them to redirect SSH traffic. “If you happen to get a circuit that exits over one of those 333 infected exit nodes, you won’t notice anything at first,” Kaba, the name of the Tor volunteer who authored the post, wrote. “Encrypted web traffic is forwarded as usual, but if you attempt an SSH connection over Port 22, you’re unknowingly redirected to one of 12 SSH interception servers. That’s exactly what happened to me on Friday morning.” Kaba then received the following warning: @@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@ @ WARNING: REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED! @ @@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@ IT IS POSSIBLE THAT SOMEONE IS DOING SOMETHING NASTY! Someone could be eavesdropping on you right now (man-in-the-middle attack)! It is also possible that a host key has just been changed. The fingerprint for the ED25519 key sent by the remote host is SHA256:6baCZCPsl+AfQuujUS81fMEE/C7UeJ+AwjrImqmDB3E. Please contact your system administrator. The volunteer investigated further and found that when people ignored the warning, the attacker (1) stole the SSH password, (2) opened a connection to the server and observed all typed commands, and (3) opened a second connection and infected the server with Ebury. To investigate the campaign, Kaba created a script that polled all of Tor’s roughly 3,000 exit nodes to check whether they received a correct SSH fingerprint from a test server. The test server then checked whether the connection came from a pinned exit node or one of 12 SSH interception servers used by the attacker. The volunteer further set up a honeypot to observe the interception software attempting to log in and install Ebury. Several mysteries remain. Tor volunteers still don’t know how the attackers accessed so many servers. One possibility is that a company hosting them was hacked, but the theory is inconclusive. Another open question is who is behind the attack. “The use of Ebury and the broad collection of unencrypted data point to a financially motivated crime group,” Kaba wrote. “Though this too is just speculation.” A last perplexing finding: why did Switzerland2 accept an RSA key when there’s no evidence it was an authorized SSH certificate? The takeaway from the event is the crucial role in-depth defense plays in preventing data theft in mass hacks like this one. Those measures include configuring email accounts to accept only encrypted traffic and reject unencrypted ports. Another is to disable password authentication in favor of SSH keys.

UN says we're going to blow past 1.5º warming, offers a "now what?" answer
2026-10-22

UN says we're going to blow past 1.5º warming, offers a "now what?" answer

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
In 2015, all but two countries agreed to make an effort to keep global temperatures from reaching 1.5° C above pre-industrial readings. Since then, most of the world has largely treated what they signed on to as aspirational, and one of the largest emitters (the US) has alternately withdrawn and recommitted to the agreement. Meanwhile, the planet has responded as expected to steadily rising carbon emissions and appears to be practically at the 1.5° limit. On Wednesday, for the first time, the United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP) acknowledged that reality, and conceded that the goal of keeping warming below 1.5° was no longer a viable one. Instead, its new goalposts focus on keeping both how long we exceed that limit and how much we exceed it by, at a minimum. Doing that will require both dramatic emissions cuts now and the removal of hundreds of gigatonnes of carbon dioxide from the atmosphere before the century is out. Depending on which baseline you use to establish pre-industrial temperatures, as well as which reconstruction of global temperatures you rely on, the world has either gotten very close to, or already exceeded the 1.5° milestone. The current El Niño event, which appears exceptionally strong, will almost certainly mean that we’ve gone over it no matter which records you choose. The UN relies on temperatures averaged over a few decades to determine where we’re at, which means we have technically not gone above the limit yet. But, as temperatures seem stubbornly disinterested in dropping much even after extremely hot years, we’re likely at the start of the period where the 1.5° goal is passed. So, while it’s not technically too late to get on a pathway that keeps us below this, practically it’s already too late. UNEP’s acknowledgment of this reality is straightforward, albeit couched in a bit of optimism. “Decades of insufficient climate action makes exceedance of 1.5° C unavoidable,” its latest report states. “Whether it will be temporary crucially depends on decisions taken today.” In other words, we might be committed to exceeding the goal, but that doesn’t necessarily mean that we’re accepting that global temperatures will need to stay above the 1.5° mark. The Paris Agreement’s means of getting to that goal was to have its signatories set emissions-reduction targets and then progressively expand those as policy tools and technology matured. Progress would be measured at key points through a “stocktake” process. But not a lot of countries followed up on what they agreed to. “While the year 2030 is drawing closer, collectively countries have still not aligned policies with [agreed to] targets,” UNEP concedes. In fact, the gap between targets and actual policy would look even worse if the US were still part of the process, instead of seeing the Trump administration pull out of the agreement a second time. A decade into the process, the needed tightening of policies has also been insufficient. In its new analysis, UNEP highlights that existing policies would likely leave us at 2.8° C above preindustrial temperatures by the end of the century, while even the stretch goals under the Paris Agreement would drop that by just 0.2°. Many countries have set net-zero goals outside of the Paris Framework, but including all of those would still leave us at 2.1° C above preindustrial temperatures by the century’s end. In all of these scenarios, temperatures would continue to rise in the ensuing century. And we don’t seem to be closer to getting our act together. Emissions were largely flat between 2024 and 2025 (there appears to be a slight decline, but within uncertainties of zero change), and emissions from burning fossil fuels were actually up, offset by better land-use management. But to have reached any significant goals, they needed to be dropping by now. But UNEP is a rare organization in that it’s willing to say the worst is not necessarily yet to come. “Every additional fraction of a degree and additional year of warming above 1.5° C raises the risk of irreversible changes including in ice sheets, permafrost carbon, ocean circulation and coral systems, along with devastating human impacts,” the report says. “The task now, therefore, is not only to ensure that global warming stays well below 2°C, but to minimize the duration and magnitude of overshoot and establish a credible return path to 1.5° C by the end of the century.” The scenario it lays out has a 66 percent chance of keeping warming to a maximum of 1.8° C above preindustrial temperatures, and to have even odds that we can have temperatures back down below 1.5° by the century’s end. The first thing that should be obvious is that this would require removing some of the emissions we are presently placing in the atmosphere. For a technology that is still in its infancy, the goals seem extreme: 320 gigatonnes of carbon dioxide pulled out of the atmosphere by the century’s end. At the same time, that’s the rough equivalent of just over five years of present emissions pulled out over decades. But that also has to be accompanied by major emissions cuts and a transition away from fossil fuels. UNEP’s scenario would cut coal use for electricity generation by 75 percent by 2035, and phase out the fuel by 2040. All uses of oil and gas would need to be cut by 30 and 35 percent, respectively, by 2040. This is needed because greenhouse gas emissions would have to drop by 70 percent between now and 2040. Again, this highlights the insufficiency of current climate pledges. UNEP determined that these would only cut emissions by about 12 percent by 2035. Put differently, by 2035, current pledges would leave us emitting 16 gigatonnes more carbon dioxide in 2035 than we would need to stay on the minimal overshoot pathway. (The gap between pledges and needs is notably smaller in the scenario that would keep warming to 2° C by century’s end, which involves a five gigatonne difference.) There are failures across the board here. While coal use is declining, it’s not projected to fall nearly fast enough over the next decade. Oil and gas need to start falling, but they’re projected to rise for a decade or more. (This report would have been in preparation before the conflict with Iran disrupted oil and gas supplies, and the data on any conservation and electrification driven by shortages is not available yet.) Solar and wind are growing fast enough for a scenario that includes reasonable conservation and efficiency efforts, but because demand is rising so quickly, they’re falling behind where we need them as a percentage of the electrical grid. The report suggests we can still limit the impact of failing to meet our climate goals. But doing so will require major efforts from many countries—the same countries that got us here by not taking the initial goals seriously enough.

FCC chair warns media not to run the "fake polls" Trump keeps complaining about
2026-10-21

FCC chair warns media not to run the "fake polls" Trump keeps complaining about

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
Federal Communications Commission Chairman Brendan Carr today warned broadcasters about airing “fake polls,” amplifying one of the many complaints that President Trump has regularly made about the news media. Carr’s post on X said, “Many journalists and broadcasters have been amplifying or airing these Polls without regard to basic journalistic ethics and without informing the public that those Polls lack a scientific, accurate, or even real basis… Broadcasters are advised to avoid engaging in News Distortions or running Broadcast Hoaxes by airing Polls that would violate their regulatory obligations.” Carr urged broadcasters to “provide their viewers with additional context and information about Polls they have covered.” In fact, one small polling outfit was exposed as a hoax this week, and a similar incident occurred in August. News outlets have reported on these hoaxes, providing the very “context” that Carr said is needed. Carr’s post did not mention any specific fake polls or any specific broadcasters that aired them. The vagueness of Carr’s post may be useful for his and Trump’s ongoing battle against the news media. In August, Trump demanded that the FCC punish or rebuke an NBC journalist for saying he has “mixed results” in election endorsements and wrote that the FCC must do something about “THE FAKE POLLS USED BY OUR CROOKED MEDIA.” Carr’s latest warning to broadcasters came as no surprise, given that he has spent much of the past two years threatening broadcast licenses and opening investigations into Trump’s least favorite news companies. Former Republican and Democratic FCC chairs and commissioners recently said Carr is seeking to censor and chill the speech of broadcasters through a proceeding that threatens the licenses of ABC-owned stations. “FCC Chair Brendan Carr’s threat against broadcasters over their reporting on political polls is yet another truly outrageous attempt to censor and control the information available to Americans as they prepare to vote… Carr is now seizing on reports about fabricated political polling as an excuse to once again try to scare broadcasters into self-censorship,” said a statement today from a group of former FCC chairs and officials. Today’s statement was attributed to former Chairman Tom Wheeler, a Democrat; former Chairman Mark Fowler, a Republican; and seven former high-ranking FCC officials. “Good-faith reporting on public polls—even ones that turn out to be made up—is not a violation of the FCC’s news distortion or hoax rules. And the FCC has zero authority to dictate editorial decisions,” they said. Wheeler, Fowler, and others previously signed a letter objecting to the ABC investigation. The letter called it “the latest attack in a sustained campaign by Chairman Carr and the Trump administration of threatening legal sanctions against licensees whose speech President Trump dislikes.” Anna Gomez, the only Democratic commissioner on the current FCC, wrote today that “Pollsters who intentionally mislead the public should answer for it, but broadcasters who report publicly available polling data should never be punished when a poll is wrong. FCC rules on news distortion or hoaxes do not apply to a station that reports this data in good faith.” An entity called Wedgewood Polls said yesterday that it published fabricated polling data “to test whether media outlets, aggregators, and commentators would report on data without verifying the source, methodology, or underlying firm legitimacy.” It claimed to be running an experiment on the modern political polling industry. “Despite having no history, formal credentials, or real data behind the vast majority of our releases, Wedgewood Polling was added to major polling aggregators, including The New York Times, FiftyPlusOne, and VoteHub, and repeatedly treated as a legitimate news source,” the statement said. Notably, the three polling aggregators mentioned in the statement are not broadcast stations or news networks whose programming is carried on local broadcast stations. That’s relevant because Carr has repeatedly said he merely uses the FCC’s regulatory authority over broadcasters with licenses to use public spectrum, and not media outlets that do not have FCC licenses. Carr’s post yesterday warned broadcasters to “comply with their public interest obligations” under “the terms of their federal licenses.” While Wedgewood Polls claimed that its data faced no scrutiny from news organizations, FiftyPlusOne said it was preparing to publish an article exposing the fraud and had sent a request for comment to Wedgewood. “They did not respond to our request, but instead posted a public statement on social media admitting that they had been fabricating data,” FiftyPlusOne wrote. FiftyPlusOne, The New York Times, and VoteHub all say they did some vetting of Wedgewood before deciding to use its polls. FiftyPlusOne wrote: Their statement alleges that FiftyPlusOne, The New York Times, and other poll aggregators did not conduct appropriate vetting of their firm prior to aggregating their polls; this is false. We reached out to them to ask who they were and confirm the methodology statement on their website. We were given two names, Jack Sims and Andrew Prater. We were able to independently confirm that Sims had previous experience in the polling industry and reviewed their methodology statement in detail. It is not atypical for a pollster to leave an existing firm, form their own, and self-fund some surveys to build reputation; we had no reason at the time to believe this case was any different. Wedgewood operated as if it were a legitimate polling firm, including collecting money from donors to conduct crowd-funded polls, and sharing raw survey data (which we now have confirmed was fraudulent) with FiftyPlusOne on Oct. 15, 2026. VoteHub Head of Data Science Zachary Donnini said that after talking to Wedgewood’s “head pollster,” Votehub “assigned them a D grade and included their polling data on our site.” Meanwhile, The New York Times updated its polling page with a statement about Wedgewood. The New York Times said it sends all new pollsters “a series of questions regarding the identities of the pollsters; the methods they were using to draw their sample, contact respondents and weight their polls; and the source of their funding.” Wedgewood answered the questions, and the NYT used 15 of its polls for state and congressional-district races. The Wedgewood “polls were never included in the tracker or average of President Trump’s approval rating or of the national polls tracking overall preference for control of Congress,” the NYT said. In the previous incident this year, a group called Median Strategies admitted it faked poll results as part of a “social experiment.” The NYT said it never included that group’s results in its trackers “because the group did not respond to our questions about its methodology.” Nate Silver removed Wedgewood from his polling site today. Wedgewood polls tended to be about 1 percentage point more favorable for Democrats than other polls, according to Silver.

Xbox wants you to pay 24 to 40 cents per hour to stream games you already bought
2026-10-21

Xbox wants you to pay 24 to 40 cents per hour to stream games you already bought

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
In September, when Microsoft severely limited the game-streaming buffet available to Game Pass subscribers, the company said non-subscribers would soon be able to “purchase cloud playtime hours through the Xbox Store and stream eligible games they own on supported devices.” Today, Microsoft rolled out those a la carte Cloud Playtime purchases to Xbox Insiders, with prices that might be a bit galling for players who have already purchased these streamable games. The “introductory launch pricing” for Xbox’s Cloud Playtime purchases is as follows: Purchased playtime “remains available until it’s used,” Microsoft writes, so you don’t have to worry about your 50-hour purchase expiring before you can use it fully. Paying for playtime directly like this evokes images of old arcades, where players would invest a quarter or two (in 20th century dollars) often for just a few minutes of gameplay. That kind of metered play has fallen heavily out of fashion, though, in favor of one-time purchases for an all-you-can-play experience at home. On the surface, paying as low as 24 cents for an hour of streaming game time doesn’t sound completely unreasonable. Remember, though, that these streaming costs come on top of whatever you paid to buy the game in the first place. Unlike an Xbox Game Pass subscription—which includes a few hours of monthly streaming access to hundreds of games as part of the subscription price—these Cloud Playtime packs “require cloud playable game purchase or, for select games, free-to-play access,” Microsoft writes. Say what you will about the old arcade model, but you didn’t have to buy the cabinet and then pay a quarter per play. Microsoft’s metered Cloud Playtime packs differ from Google’s failed Stadia streaming experiment, which offered unlimited streaming playtime across devices for games you purchased on the platform. Of course, Stadia game purchases also didn’t let you download games for local play on a PC or console, making the overall value proposition quite different. That was especially true after Stadia’s 2022 shutdown made those purchases unplayable (though Google offered full refunds for any and all Stadia purchases at that point). In 2014, Sony offered a la carte streaming game access for set rental periods as part of its new PlayStation Now service. While that system didn’t require up-front game purchases, the initial pricing was exorbitant, rising to $30 for 90 days of real-time streaming access, even for games that could be purchased outright on disc for less. Those early streaming price experiments quickly gave way to an all-you-can-stream PlayStation Now subscription plan, which has now morphed into unlimited streaming of select PlayStation games through the top-tier, $20/month (or $160/year) PlayStation Plus Premium plan. Microsoft is also reminding cloud-curious gamers that Xbox Insiders can test free, ad-supported streams of games they own without buying any playtime packs. That might be the best option for gamers who want to test out a quick Halo session on their phone, rather than maintaining regular streaming access to their Xbox libraries. But Microsoft also says that paid users will get “shorter wait times [and] high-quality streaming,” meaning a deteriorated experience for those who use the ad-supported tier. In the end, it’s a question of how much of a mass market there is for Xbox Cloud Streaming on its own, rather than as an outgrowth of Xbox Game Pass subscriptions. Last month, Microsoft said that only 4 percent of Game Pass subscribers would be affected by the subscription’s new limits of five to 15 hours of streaming playtime per month. That suggests only a small minority of Xbox users want to use cloud streaming for a significant portion of their gaming time, even when it was included as a free perk with their existing subscription. How much less interest will there be among non-subscribers who have to pay by the hour for streaming access to games they already own?
Ars Technica - All content
Enterprises keep buying software upgrades that IT thinks will make things worse
2026-10-21

Enterprises keep buying software upgrades that IT thinks will make things worse

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
Enterprise-sized businesses spend, on average, 28 percent of their software budgets on unwanted upgrades, migrations, and licensing changes. Often, those firms’ IT heads think those changes are detrimental. Those results are according to a survey of 2,018 chief information officers and senior IT decision-makers at companies with at least 10,000 employees that Origina commissioned for its report, “The Software Tax.” About half of the respondents (1,011) are from the US, and the rest are from the United Kingdom. All respondents worked at companies with at least $1 billion in annual revenue. Keep in mind that Origina provides maintenance for various enterprise software. Its report points to independent software maintenance as a way for IT leaders to regain control over software budgets, illustrating its potential bias. A third-party research consultancy, Censuswide, conducted the survey. Among the report’s most concerning findings, enterprises often make software purchases that IT heads believe could hurt the organization. Ninety-five percent of respondents said “their organization has paid for a vendor-driven upgrade, migration, or licensing change in the past three years that it believed at the time would reduce performance, reliability, or business value,” the report says. Fifty-three percent of respondents “have done so more than once,” the report notes. “Some 76 percent say their organization has very little real leverage in negotiations, and 80 percent say moving away from major vendors would cause significant disruption,” the report says, referring to the share of respondents who selected “strongly agree” or “somewhat agree” to those statements. “When the alternative to an unwanted upgrade is losing support on systems the business runs on, saying yes is often a rational response,” Tomás O’Leary, one of the report’s authors and Origina’s CEO and founder, said in a statement. “That’s exactly the problem. The market has left some of the most experienced technology leaders in the world with little room to refuse, and that cost shows up in budgets, in engineering time, and in innovation that never gets funded.” The result is less funding for other areas, particularly, per the report, innovation (named by 36 percent of respondents), human resources (32 percent), security (31 percent), and “customer-facing investment” (31 percent). In a statement accompanying the survey’s announcement, Liberty Mutual’s CIO, James McGlennon, pointed out that an upgrade cycle could occupy top technical workers for “the better part of a year.” “That’s not a one-off cost, it’s a recurring drag on capacity, and every year it repeats is a year those people aren’t working on the initiatives that actually differentiate the business,” he said. The majority (91 percent) surveyed would support laws that give enterprises “protections comparable with those available to long-term residential tenants.” Only 1 percent opposed this (with the rest signaling that they neither support nor oppose the idea). CIOs should “negotiate the exit before the entry” to have more control over software budgets, Marshall Van Alstyne, the report’s co-author and an information sciences professor at Boston University, said in a statement. Origina’s report suggests “settling the terms of a potential exit,” including setting a price for migration assistance and caps on renewal and expansion prices during initial negotiations with the software vendor. Origina’s report didn’t call out any specific vendors as forcing enterprises’ hands. However, every survey respondent currently runs “at least one of IBM, HCL, VMware/Broadcom, or SAP at meaningful scale.”
Fortune | FORTUNE
AI tools discovered the PHP vulnerability that could take down 70% of the internet. Now, the race is on to patch it before AI-powered hackers attack
2026-10-21

AI tools discovered the PHP vulnerability that could take down 70% of the internet. Now, the race is on to patch it before AI-powered hackers attack

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
Increasingly powerful AI tools are at the center of a new war between those who want to use them to supercharge cyberattacks, and the defenders who are harnessing the same tools to fend them off. Today, the defenders are hoping they got a leg up in identifying a critical issue that stands to take down 70% of the internet if left unaddressed.While the software flaws have now been found and patches issued, the question remains whether enough of the internet will deploy the patches in time, before hackers start exploiting the vulnerability to attack websites and steal vital data. Most web pages run using an open-source programming language called PHP. That language is maintained by the PHP Foundation. Today, the foundation disclosed two “critical” software vulnerabilities and issued an emergency patch, urging websites to implement it immediately or take down their websites until they are able to. The vulnerabilities are named CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619 and collectively called “php2shell.” As reports broke late on Oct. 20 of the vulnerabilities ahead of the patch’s official release, some opportunistic attackers tried to exploit it. Cloudflare, a global cybersecurity company that helps secure millions of websites, saw “tens of thousands” of exploit attempts within 45 minutes of the word getting out. That number jumped to “hundreds of thousands” within two hours, and millions by the evening of Oct. 21, according to seeing “early attacks,” according to its co-founder and CEO Matthew Prince. Amazon Web Services also reported on Oct. 21 that the vulnerability is “now being broadly exploited in the wild.” The company said that it had seen some attempts by hackers to use the exploit in the days prior to the vulnerability being disclosed on Oct. 20, raising the possibility that the news about the flaw had leaked to hackers prior to the official announcement from PHP. One startup owner reported that the PHP bug is “real bad” and says everyone should be “afraid” after hackers allegedly stole their source code, exposed cookie data, and leaked legal documents. Multiple governments have decided to take websites offline as a safety precaution, including some agencies in the Netherlands and Finland, Reuters reported. The FBI’s cyber division and the National Security Agency both issued statements urging potentially affected organizations to update their services, as did the prime minister of Japan, the European Union’s computer emergency response team, and several other national agencies. “The impact of the PHP vulnerabilities is massive, [and] can expose a large number of websites to immediate attacks if not patched in time,” Gang Wang, associate professor of computer science at the University of Illinois’s Grainger College of Engineering, told Fortune. The bugs allow bad actors to remotely execute code on flawed, not-yet-patched PHP systems. Attackers can use that as an “entry point into the network hosting that machine, and from there potentially carry out attacks that cause significant damage,” Wang’s colleague, David Nicol, director of the Information Trust Institute at Grainger College, told Fortune. Among the most well known sites that run on PHP is WordPress, which powers roughly 40% of all websites, according to Reuters. But PHP is ubiquitous across the web, having started as a small project back in 1994 and grown up with the modern internet. For example, Facebook launched in 2004 as a PHP website, although in 2020 the company embarked on a major effort to update to different software. Other popular sites using PHP include Microsoft.com, Wikipedia.org, Archive.org (now temporarily offline), TheGuardian.com, and Baidu.com, according to W3Techs. An initial analysis by cybersecurity firm Palo Alto Networks identified 22.6 million potentially vulnerable instances. OpenAI cofounder and president Greg Brockman said PHP was his “first programming language,” in a post about the vulnerability discovery. OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol model, as well as other AI tools, played a key role in finding the vulnerabilities. “Given the severity of these flaws and PHP’s widespread use, we consider this one of the most impactful security issues in recent memory,” OpenAI said in a blog post. “It highlights how AI can uncover security flaws with internet-scale impact, but the same capabilities can also help attackers rapidly develop offensive tools and exploits.” Multiple security organizations independently found the issue around the same time, in August. All of them used AI to help find the vulnerabilities. They then banded together in a coordinated effort with the PHP Foundation to develop fixes and coordinate the disclosure of the vulnerabilities and rollout of the patch, OpenAI said. Technically, the first to discover part of the issue was the California-based AI red-teaming firm Calif. OpenAI had invited Calif to join its Patch the Planet initiative, an effort to proactively find and patch security vulnerabilities in digital infrastructure. Calif decided to investigate PHP because it “stood out as a high-impact target,” the company says. It reached out to Volker Dusch at the PHP Foundation, and then “got to work hacking PHP.” The Calif team was “not expecting to find anything critical” since PHP has been highly scrutinized by the cybersecurity community for years. In fact, the bug they eventually found had “been present in PHP for 14 years,” OpenAI’s Brockman pointed out in his post. “A limited version of it had been discovered in 2024 and wasn’t considered significant at the time.” But now that AI models can run attack sequences autonomously, almost no vulnerability is too difficult to exploit. “Php2shell shows how AI can help uncover buried flaws and turn complex exploit chains into practical attacks, increasing the potential risk,” TJ Sayers, senior director of threat intelligence at the Center for Internet Security, told Fortune. On Aug. 1, Calif found the first issue using GPT-5.6 Sol. It then informed OpenAI, which looked into the issue on its own and found additional elements of the bug. This made the discovery more comprehensive, but almost made clear that the flaw was more serious than Calif had originally realized, an OpenAI spokesperson told Fortune. Then OpenAI began chatting with the PHP Foundation about the issue. Through those conversations, OpenAI learned that Wiz, a Google-owned cybersecurity company, was also talking to the foundation about the issue. Researchers at Wiz had independently found the vulnerabilities in August. They decided to investigate PHP in the first place because it’s “the backbone of the internet,” a Wiz spokesperson tells Fortune, acknowledging that the parallel initiative may seem like a “coincidence,” but the Wiz team’s charter is to investigate high-impact digital infrastructure, and PHP was top of mind. The Wiz team identified the first issue on Aug. 19, using a combination of Google’s Gemini 4 Argon, OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol, and Wiz Atlas. “With 70% of the internet affected, the impact of php2shell cannot be overstated,” Wiz said in a blog post announcing its findings. “It marks an AI inflection point. The vulnerable code had been there for more than a decade–what AI changed was the ability to understand the weaknesses and turn them into a working exploit.” OpenAI then decided to bring all the parties together, leading a “coalition of maintainers, researchers, and security providers to address [the] critical vulnerabilities,” the company said. That included Wiz, Calif, PHP, Cloudflare, and multiple U.S. government agencies, among others. Cloudflare’s blog post also names the French cybersecurity company Quarkslab as part of the effort. Update, Oct. 22: This story has been updated to include Cloudflare’s early data on the number of exploit attempts after the news got out, and comment from Amazon Web Services about hackers use of the exploit. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. $3 menu items, a $4 breakfast, and fewer visits: McDonald’s barrage of deals isn’t winning over enough customers
2026-10-07

$3 menu items, a $4 breakfast, and fewer visits: McDonald’s barrage of deals isn’t winning over enough customers

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
The menu items for under $3 didn’t do the trick. Even the comparatively popular $4 breakfast deal that included a Sausage McMuffin wasn’t enough. McDonald’s CEO Chris Kempczinski has been trying to persuade customers squeezed by inflation or tempted away by competitors’ burgers and chicken sandwiches to return to the Golden Arches. But so far, the company’s barrage of new meal deals and aggressive marketing to promote them have come up short. In each quarter this year, McDonald’s U.S. growth has shrunk, and customer visits reportedly dropped last quarter. Wall Street has taken notice: McDonald’s shares are now down 32% from an all-time high in February. What’s worse, some of its efforts to repair those downward trends have backfired. Kempczinski conceded in August that some changes to its offerings, such as removing its Buy One, Add One for $1 feature, uneven execution across the restaurant chain, and often insufficient marketing, had hurt its efforts. The impact on service and sales irked franchisees, a crucial constituency, just as McDonald’s was asking them to spend hundreds of thousands of dollars per store on remodels and upgrades. The CEO defended his plan this summer, arguing that the strategy was good, and only the execution was bad. But McDonald’s paltry results of late suggest there is more to its malaise than just a few tactical errors. In its most recent quarter, McDonald’s reported U.S. comparable sales growth of 0.8%, hardly a catastrophe but a continuation of its slowing growth and an underwhelming performance, especially compared with the 8.5% jump at resurgent rival Burger King. “We must be the first choice for more customers more often,” Kempczinski told analysts at McDonald’s investor day at its Chicago headquarters in September. There, he fleshed out the restaurant chain’s global business strategy, called “McDonald’s > Next,” which aims to serve better food, improve service, and make restaurants easier to run. But investors seem skeptical; for now they see more tarnish than gleam in the Golden Arches. What’s worried investors most about McDonald’s financial performance, particularly in the U.S. where it generates 40% of revenue or $10 billion a year, is a drop in store visits. While McDonald’s doesn’t report traffic numbers, analytics firm Placer.ai estimates U.S. visits fell 4.5% in the second quarter of 2026. (A company spokesperson said McDonald’s is “unable to corroborate or validate third-party data.”) McDonald’s risks losing more customers if it has to raise prices to contend with beef inflation. The U.S. Department of Agriculture reports that beef prices in August were 5.9% higher than a year earlier. Kempczinski said last month that beef costs had nearly doubled over the past five years in the company’s biggest markets, an acute problem for McDonald’s given its lower-income clientele. He’s said price hikes are on the table, but the company lost customers after it raised prices during the COVID crisis, a lesson etched in his mind. Plenty of quick-service restaurant chains, notably Wendy’s, Popeyes, and Papa John’s, are also struggling in this environment. But there are winners in the sector, and those that are thriving are doing so by remodeling their restaurants for efficiency and refreshing their menus. Case in point is Burger King, owned by Canadian conglomerate Restaurant Brands International. As part of its “Reclaim the Flame” turnaround plan (a decidedly catchier name than “McDonald’s > Next”), Burger King has overhauled half its restaurant fleet in four years with more to come. Its Whopper sales have jumped 20% this year after the chain revamped its flagship burger for the first time in a decade by upgrading its bun, mayo, and packaging. Burger King’s marketing has also been particularly deft: When Kempczinski awkwardly bit into the Big Arch burger, which he called a “product,” in a promotional spot, Burger King countered with President Tom Curtis eating the Whopper in a natural, relaxed way. To be sure, McDonald’s remains the dominant fast-food operator, with 11% of the U.S. fast-food market, according to a ranking in QSR magazine. Its U.S. sales are five times those of Burger King, the second largest hamburger-oriented chain in the U.S. Still, McDonald’s is feeling the pressure. “To succeed … growth must come from capturing greater (market) share,” Kempczinski told investors. Renovating restaurants is part of his plan to lure customers back to McDonald’s, but to do that he needs buy-in from franchisees, who own 95% of all restaurants. Under McDonald’s agreement with franchisees, store operators pay the fast food giant rent, fees, and royalties, and the parent company gets a strong say in restaurant locations and standards. Franchisees can opt out of McDonald’s directives, and, indeed, just 60% to 65% of restaurants implemented its “under $3 menu” this summer amid fears of lower margins. McDonald’s recently pushed back its target of renovating 50,000 total restaurants to 2028 from the 2027 deadline it set in 2023. The CEO has said McDonald’s is having “constructive” discussions with franchisees to get them on board. At investor day, Kempczinski announced an $8.5 billion 10-year plan to help franchisees with capital support and rent relief as they contend with higher food, labor, and equipment costs. Some $5 billion of that sum will be deployed by 2030. Shares fell on news of the spending, but BTIG’s Peter Saleh said in a note that it was key for McDonald’s to align franchisees behind the next-generation upgrades, according to Benzinga. UBS analysts praised McDonald’s plan as “achievable with solid execution,” and said it was “critical to franchisees’ health and their ability to reinvest long term.” When it comes to its menu, McDonald’s hasn’t been asleep at the wheel; in fact, it’s been experimenting a lot. At the top of its priority list on investor day was winning market share in the surging chicken and beverage markets. The company has been testing hand-breaded chicken and, for users of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, protein-rich grilled chicken bowls in the U.S. It also introduced a new line of crafted sodas and energy drinks with Red Bull. McDonald’s aims to raise its market share in the global beverage and chicken markets by 1.5 percentage points each by 2030. Like every other major company, McDonald’s is looking for ways AI can improve its operations and service. It is weighing giving restaurant workers access to chatbots and testing voice-AI ordering at the drive-thru. Such plans could give the Golden Arches an edge; Deutsche Bank’s Lauren Silberman wrote that AI implementation could improve the efficiency and profitability of McDonald’s restaurants in a way that would be harder for smaller players to replicate. Kempczinski, who joined McDonald’s in 2015 as a top executive in charge of strategy and innovation, is generally well regarded by Wall Street and has navigated the periodic slumps that come with the industry. His previous multiyear strategy, called “Accelerating the Arches,” was a success, increasing the number of orders placed digitally; building McDonald’s loyalty program membership; and culminating in several years of sales growth. Still, the CEO may have a smaller margin for error now. “We’re committed to making sure that our restaurants don’t fall behind like they did previously,” he told investors. CORRECTION: The story and headline were updated to make clear which McDonald’s promotions were performing poorly. SoFi becomes first consumer bank to launch tokenized deposits, offers 4.4% interest
2026-10-22

SoFi becomes first consumer bank to launch tokenized deposits, offers 4.4% interest

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
The convergence between traditional and blockchain-based worlds of finance continues to accelerate. The latest example came on Thursday when the personal finance company SoFi announced it is offering tokenized deposits, which are functionally equivalent to cash but are stored on a blockchain. The new product is called SoFi Earn, or SOFIE, and those who hold the tokenized deposits will earn a 4.4% rate of interest and benefit from FDIC protection. The launch comes amid an ongoing, and often confusing, debate over the merits of tokenized deposits versus stablecoins, which are another form of dollar equivalents long embraced by the crypto industry. The two types of digital dollar are worth the same, but are subject to different regulatory regimes and use cases. Stablecoins like USDC, which is owned by Circle and Coinbase, are backed by a 1-to-1 reserve of dollars or U.S. Treasuries and can be transferred to anyone in the world who holds a crypto wallet. But they are a form of currency that, for now at least, exists outside the U.S. banking system, and are ineligible to earn interest or receive backing from the FDIC. This has led banks like Citi to create tokenized deposits for institutional customers as a way to capture some of the benefits of blockchain, such as instant settlement and 24/7 money movement, while still operating within the confines of the existing banking regime. SoFi, which holds a federal banking charter, is the first to do so at the consumer level. Tokenized deposits, however, can only be used to move money if both parties have a relationship with the bank that created them—a big limitation in the view of stablecoin advocates. It’s not immediately obvious why SoFi is offering tokenized deposits given that its customers can already earn a competitive interest rate from the company’s savings account product, and also buy its SoFiUSD stablecoin. But in an interview with Fortune, CEO Anthony Noto said SoFi Earn will appeal to crypto users—who often park their money in stablecoins while riding out market ups and downs—looking to earn yield. “I say to my team ‘money doesn’t sleep’ doesn’t mean it just moves 24/7, but it also needs to be making money 24/7,” he said. Noto explained that, in these cases, SoFi customers will be able to buy Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies using their tokenized deposits. He also described crypto companies as “low hanging fruit” when it comes to those who would embrace SoFi Earn, explaining that they will be able to pay Mastercard and other service providers that also have accounts with SoFi. In the bigger picture, says Noto, SoFi’s decision to offer tokenized deposits is a way to add another piece to its growing suite of financial services. He added that, for practical purposes, this means that individuals and companies will be able to easily toggle between legacy networks like ACH and wire transfers and blockchain-based options—all in a single interface. The launch of SoFi Earn comes after the recent collapse in Congress of a major piece of crypto legislation known as the Clarity Act. The bill foundered in large part over a provision that would have allowed companies to pay interest on stablecoins. The banking industry has argued that such a provision could pose an existential threat to financial stability—while the crypto industry and others have claimed the real reason for the banks’ opposition is to protect their current business model, which often pays consumers virtually nothing for the money in their accounts. Jeff John Roberts is the Finance and Crypto editor at Fortune, overseeing coverage of the blockchain and how technology is changing finance. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fintech startup Coast raises $35 million to better track what workers do with company credit cards
2026-10-22

Fintech startup Coast raises $35 million to better track what workers do with company credit cards

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Getting the job done means trusting workers to spend company money—and keeping track of where it goes. In industries like plumbing and construction, employees often carry company cards to buy fuel and supplies, while office staff sort through receipts to check that spending stays within bounds. Coast, a New York-based startup, wants to make this process more transparent by pairing company payment cards with software that helps businesses control spending and track purchases made on the job. On Thursday, the company, founded in 2020 by former Wall Street engineer Daniel Simon, announced a $35 million growth equity round that closed in October. It plans to use the funding to build out a platform that helps businesses manage employee spending, facilitate payments to vendors, and automate accounting tasks. “If you run a plumbing business or an HVAC… and you need to give the technician who works for you the company credit card when they take the van out to go to their job… you need really specialized control, security, visibility, [and] reporting,” Simon, who serves as Coast’s CEO, told Fortune. It works like this: A worker picks up a company card and checks in by text or app. That links the card to their identity and the spending limits their employer has set. Coast can then use vehicle location data to check whether the company van is at the gas station where the card is used—and block the purchase if it isn’t. Once a purchase goes through, the software sends the transaction details to the company’s accounting system, so office staff have fewer records to enter and match by hand. Thomvest Ventures led the growth equity round, joined by new investors Intuit Ventures and Small World Capital. Returning backers included ICONIQ Growth, Accel, Insight Partners, Better Tomorrow Ventures, Avid Ventures, Vintage Investment Partners, Synchrony, RXR, and The Fintech Fund. The funding follows Coast’s Series B, which raised $40 million, and an earlier $92 million funding round. Coast declined to disclose its latest valuation. Though artificial intelligence promises to automate office work, its growth depends on workers who build data centers and upgrade power grids. U.S. demand for skilled trades is growing three times faster than for desk jobs, with openings for heating, ventilation, and air conditioning technicians up 78% and broader trade postings up 30%, according to a March report by staffing company Randstad USA. Its research also found that 102 workers leave the trades for every 100 who enter, putting pressure on businesses to do more with fewer people. Coast sees an opportunity to sell payment tools to these businesses in a market it estimates at $120 billion. “You are taking time that might have otherwise been spent [on] back office work and giving that back to the people in the trades to work on what they are really focused on,” Don Butler, managing director at Thomvest Ventures, told Fortune. Simon first saw an opportunity to tap into this market during the height of the pandemic, shortly after he sold his previous payments startup, Bread, for $500 million in 2020. The sale marked the latest milestone in a career that began on Wall Street, where Simon built trading software for investment banks and hedge funds before earning law and business degrees from Yale University. But while banks and tech companies used modern software, he saw plumbers, electricians, and heating and cooling technicians rely on payment tools designed before smartphones existed. Today, Coast has about 135 employees across its New York headquarters and a Utah office, and serves thousands of businesses, including Tristate Plumbing, Texas Solar Outfitters, and Verde Property Services. The company is not yet profitable and says it prioritizes growth, with revenue reinvested in its products and efforts to attract customers. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
Elon Musk’s dream merger is a nightmare for SpaceX shareholders
2026-10-22

Elon Musk’s dream merger is a nightmare for SpaceX shareholders

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
The blowout news from SpaceX’s Q3 earnings wasn’t the rocket and AI giant’s outstanding performance that far exceeded even the analysts’ elevated expectations, but Elon Musk’s revelation that SpaceX and Tesla are weighing a merger. On the call, held the evening of October 20, Musk declared that his two signature holdings have each named “special committees” composed of independent directors to explore a union. Musk’s statement confirmed Wall Street’s long-held view that the deal was highly likely, and now—given that Musk effectively controls both companies and loves the idea (though as a “conflicted party” can’t serve on the committees), the tie-up looks all but inevitable. The prospect of a SpaceX-Tesla combo got rave notices from the research crews covering both companies. In most cases where the acquirer is en route to paying a price amounting to a humongous PE, its stock falls. Not this time. Investors applauded both enterprises, sending Tesla up 3.4% the day following Musk’s statement and SpaceX up 7.8%, adding $155 billion in market cap. At first glance, their already tight kinship, and all their many overlapping businesses, suggest that a new SpaceX-Tesla makes sense. SpaceX buys Tesla Megapack batteries and Cybertrucks, Tesla invested $2 billion in xAI, bought by SpaceX in February, and they share ownership of the much-feted chip project Terafab. Not to mention that the deal would clinch Musk’s long-harbored vision of running a single colossus as a kind of Berkshire Hathaway of AI-driven tech. The wizard freed to blend these two great elixirs of innovation? What’s not to like? A close examination of claims made for the deal, especially assumptions on its supposed advantages in bolstering the finances of both companies, points to an extremely risky wager that must perform never-before-seen heroics to reward shareholders. It’s the ultimate, buy the leap of faith, it’s about the vision, not the numbers, never bet against Elon scenario, set against the sobering numbers. Let’s explore four frequently made arguments for what would prove the largest and, so far, probably the most universally praised merger in the annals of capitalism. The idea is that while SpaceX profits are just starting to accelerate, adding Tesla’s earnings, generated from its bedrock EV and battery sales, would immediately raise the former’s EPS. In fact, it’s technically true: If the pair combined now, that’s how the math would work. The problem: The addition to SpaceX’s GAAP profits per share would be minuscule, and since SpaceX’s bottom line’s growing and Tesla’s dropping, the deal if done a year or two from now would prove extremely dilutive for the Starlink provider. What stands out when you examine what SpaceX would pay versus what earnings it would get is how shockingly expensive the transaction would be. One big question: How much premium would SpaceX offer over Tesla’s current price? As Rajat Gupta of JPMorgan points out in a report on the likely merger, that number in most tech acquisitions is 30% to 35%. So let’s posit 30%. Right now, Tesla’s market cap is $1.52 trillion; tack on 30%, and the transaction would cost SpaceX $1.98 trillion SpaceX’s valuation stands at $2.14 trillion. We’re looking at almost a merger of equals. SpaceX would need to issue the equivalent of 92% of its current shares outstanding to capture Tesla. Hence, SpaceX’s count would zoom from 13.1 billion to 25 billion shares. SpaceX would be awarding nearly half of its future earnings to Tesla’s selling shareholders. And what’s it getting in return? We don’t know what the robots and self-driving cars will deliver, but we do know that Tesla booked just $3.28 billion in GAAP net profits over the past four quarters. Since SpaceX had a $1.5 billion loss over that period, the pro forma deal, as of today, would be “accretive” in the sense that buying Tesla would make the combined company slightly profitable. But SpaceX would just go from EPS that’s a minuscule negative to a tiny positive. What’s important is the amount SpaceX is paying for those earnings and puny “accretion.” It would be getting $3.28 billion in profits in exchange for stock worth $1.98 trillion. That’s $600 for every $1 in extra earnings. To make matters worse, Tesla’s profits have been falling while SpaceX’s bottom line is taking off. In Q3, SpaceX reversed the three prior quarters of deficits by registering $4.4 billion in GAAP net profits, enough to lower its 4-quarter loss to that $1.5 billion. In other words, profits are trending the right way, big time. Via a merger, SpaceX would be sacrificing half of those fast-rising earnings to the Tesla shareholders, and getting what’s for now a minimal contribution from Tesla. On the “accretion” argument, it looks like a bad outcome for SpaceX shareholders. Even if SpaceX didn’t buy Tesla, its valuation is so gigantic alongside its earnings that only by achieving stupendous growth in revenues and profits can it deliver shareholders an even decent return. In May, David Trainer of New Constructs provided math reckoning that at its current valuation of $2.14 trillion, SpaceX would need to be generating around $300 billion in profits on sales of roughly $1.6 trillion by 2035 to provide a competitive return. That’s a tall order, to put it mildly. The profits number would be almost a quarter more than what Alphabet makes now, on over three times its revenues. The rub: A Tesla acquisition simply gives SpaceX a far steeper hill to climb, one that’s almost twice as steep. If SpaceX shoulders a 30% premium, the combined companies would start life with a market cap of $4.12 trillion. (That’s SpaceX’s current $2.14 trillion plus the $1.98 trillion paid for Tesla.) SpaceX 2.0 would edge Alphabet ($4.0 trillion) and Microsoft ($3.9 trillion) to rank as the third-most-valuable enterprise in America behind only Nvidia ($5.3 trillion). and Apple ($5.0 trillion). By Fortune’s estimate, using Trainer’s methodology, SpaceX would need $550 billion in profits and around $3 trillion in revenue to ring the bell by 2035. That $550 billion target is over twice what Alphabet amasses today. Once again, if Tesla were bringing already big and fast-rising profits to the table, the task wouldn’t be nearly as difficult. But it’s just the opposite. The EV-maker’s providing exceedingly slender profits that have already declined from $15 billion in 2023, and $5 billion in 2024 to a run rate that two-thirds the figure of just two years ago. Much of the attention on SpaceX’s financials focuses on its incredible cash burn that’s chiefly financing its ambitions in AI, or what it now calls “Super Intelligence.” In the first nine months of this year, SpaceX pocketed $13.1 billion in cash from operations, and lavished $48.1 billion in capex, of which $40 billion went to AI projects, chiefly the buildup in terrestrial data campuses and what it sees as tomorrow’s cash cow, AI centers in space, for negative free cash flow of $15 billion. What’s more, CFO Bret Johnsen disclosed on the earnings call plans for $35 billion to $40 billon in investment just for Q4 alone. SpaceX is seeking to raise an extra $40 billion in debt to help fund the campaign, but even fans on Wall Street question how SpaceX will attract the massive sums required going forward, estimated by Morgan Stanley at $230 billion over the next three years. In its previous incarnation as an EV specialist, Tesla could have helped. Until this year, it typically provided large amounts of free cash flow. But now that Musk’s repurposed Tesla as a robotics and self-driving franchise, it’s in a similar position to SpaceX though on a lesser scale. In April, Musk had shocked the markets by declaring that Tesla would spend an immense $25 billion on capex this year. Indeed, the number ramped rapidly, and Tesla registered negative free cash flow in Q2. But Q3 was a surprise. Capex came in lower than expected, as Tesla’s cash from operations exceeded its investments by $1.1 billion. Musk also disclosed that Tesla was cutting its capex target for the year from $25 billion to $20 billion. Still, that goal implies $7 billion in capex for the final quarter of this year, a big number almost certain to at least match, if not exceed, cash from operations. On the call, Tesla CFO Vaibhav Taneja stated that investment spending will accelerate in the years ahead to back expansion of the robotaxi fleet, build capacity for production of Optimus robots, and ramp AI infrastructure capacity, all pointing a likely, long period of negative free cash flow. Upshot: Tesla is no longer generating free cash flow, and given its own big investment plans for robots and robotaxis, can’t help plug SpaceX huge cash flow deficits. So putting SpaceX and Tesla together unites two companies that are super-hungry for cash. If you simply extrapolate the numbers, it’s likely that SpaceX and Tesla combined will spend close to a staggering $50 billion on capex in Q4, and their businesses won’t come close to covering that number. Looking forward, adding Tesla will simply make a new SpaceX’s huge need for cash far more acute. This one gets a ringing endorsement. Tesla’s shares now look vastly overvalued versus the basics. At its current market cap of $1.52 trillion, Tesla is selling at a PE of 633. Shareholders are getting one-sixth of a penny in earnings for every dollar they own in Tesla shares. And Tesla’s core, repeatable profits are actually lower than the reported numbers. In many quarters, they drop considerably when you back out sales of regulatory credits, a category Musk himself says will disappear, and unrealized gains on its SpaceX stake. So what explains this phenomenon where profits keep falling and the market cap remains in the stratosphere? As investor Steve Eisman of The Big Short fame explained to me in a recent story, it’s the prospect of a full-price buyout from SpaceX that’s supporting Tesla shares. Says Trainer, “A SpaceX deal is the only way to bail out Tesla shareholders.” So if SpaceX indeed buys Tesla, it’s the Tesla owners who will prove the winners. And that’s the outcome they’re counting on. The losers will be SpaceX’s current shareholders that trade almost half their company for puny profits and big promises. Fortune | FORTUNE
Steven Bartlett started ‘The Diary of a CEO’ with a $100 microphone. Now he says it’s on track for $60 million in annual revenue
2026-10-20

Steven Bartlett started ‘The Diary of a CEO’ with a $100 microphone. Now he says it’s on track for $60 million in annual revenue

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-20

Visit Original Website
Steven Bartlett started his podcast with just a laptop and a sock pulled over a $100 microphone. Before there were guests, it was just Bartlett, reading from his diary at 2 a.m. and trying to make sense of what he had written that week. He eventually began interviewing other people about theirs. Now, The Diary of a CEO is one of the world’s biggest podcasts and is on track to overtake Joe Rogan as the most-subscribed podcast on YouTube. The show has grown into a business Bartlett forecasts will generate about $60 million in revenue this year. But growing it has also meant turning down money—including a deal that would have required more advertising and taking the show off YouTube. He described the proposed arrangement as a trade: Accept a large payment, put 12 advertisements in the podcast, and have little say over those ads. “One of the central reasons why I turned down such an offer is because that is four times more ads than I have to put in now,” Bartlett told Fortune’s Ellie Austin on Fortune Daily. Working with what he calls “fragmentation vultures”—companies seeking exclusive podcast distribution deals—can mean sacrificing revenue or control over the relationship with an audience, Bartlett argued. Instead, he sees the relationship with his audience as worth protecting, even when a lucrative offer like that comes along. The business he aims to preserve already makes money beyond advertising through products, subscriptions, and events. Bartlett, who dropped out of college to pursue entrepreneurship, launched The Diary of a CEO in 2017 by turning his own diary entries into podcast episodes. He later began interviewing other people about their own diaries, finding it more intellectually stimulating to learn about their own lives than to read about his own. Those conversations explored his guests’ motivations, insecurities, and ambitions, eventually broadening the show’s scope to subjects ranging from neuroscience and psychology to performance and sports. And with it, the business has expanded too. When asked where the show’s projected $60 million comes from, Bartlett pointed to brand partnerships, products his business owns and manufactures, subscriptions, memberships, and events. On top of that, he runs an investment fund. “It’s a wide range of things and again, this goes to the point of diversification,” he said. Steven.com, Bartlett’s holding company, houses the podcast and his other creator businesses. It also includes other creators, helping them build media brands and develop products and ventures. Bartlett forecasts about $90 million in revenue for his broader media company this year, including the projected $60 million from The Diary of a CEO. He also put next year’s holding-company forecast at about $160 million. But he’s not the only person to see nine-figure valuations as a result of the podcasting business: Call Her Daddy podcast host Alex Cooper’s holding company Unwell raised investment at a $500 million valuation in August. Bartlett sees that evolution as part of a wider shift of how creators work with brands. Early in his career, he said companies treated influencers like billboards: Pay them, and they would post something. Then came the creative-director titles, equity arrangements, and opportunities to cofound businesses. That is most evident with Sydney Sweeney, who became an equity partner and creative director at sports prediction market Novig, shaping its advertising rather than just simply appearing in it. He believes the next stage involves creator businesses making acquisitions and operating more like large holding companies. “You’re no longer an influencer, you’re no longer an equity partner, a creative director,” he said. “You are an owner or cofounder.” Distribution has become a point of negotiation across podcasting, perhaps most famously in 2024, when Spotify ended Rogan’s exclusivity and allowed his show to stream on rival platforms. Bartlett prefers fewer partnerships, and only with businesses that matter to him personally. About 70% of his podcast partners, he said, are companies whose founders he approached himself. Choosing sponsors is only one part of maintaining audience trust—what guests say on the podcast matters too. On Fortune Daily, he was questioned about a 2024 BBC investigation into health claims made on his show. He said his team had contracted a full-time medical PhD to review health episodes, adding context on screen and removing material that could not adequately be contextualized. Now, an independent review team fact-checks every episode—not just those focused on health—before it is published. When asked whether an unsupported scientific statement would be removed from the audio, he said the decision depends on its framing: A definitive claim would be treated differently from a guest discussing a hypothesis based on early research. But despite his growing influence, Bartlett doesn’t consider himself a journalist. He described his interviews as an exercise in pursuing his own curiosity, whether he’s sitting across from Kamala Harris or JD Vance. Viewers will always have questions they wish he had asked, he said, but he believes his approach needs to remain anchored in that curiosity. “If you’re not anchored to, like, your own curiosity, you’re going to be a plastic bag in the wind,” he said. As the business continues to grow, Bartlett’s advice to other creators is to think beyond the next deal—while acknowledging that his own success gives him more freedom to choose. “If you want to build a durable business in this creator economy, you have to prioritize sovereignty,” he said. “You’re renting your audience at the moment.” Tatiana Sataua is a News Fellow at Fortune covering business, technology, AI, and consumer culture. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Roughly 60pc of the reductions will come from within support functions, impacting developers and other professionals. Bank and financial services company Barclays has announced it expects to cut 5,000 jobs, representing around 5pc of the bank’s total headcount. The layoffs are part of Barclay’s wider plan to cut costs using artificial intelligence. Reportedly, around 60pc of the reductions are coming from within Barclay’s support functions, with developers among those expected to be impacted. Of the 5,000 roles being eliminated, 1,000 were already cut in the first half of the year, with the remainder to go by the end of the year. They relate both to the simplification of the organisation and to some of the impacts that we’re starting to see from AI.” Barclays is nearing the culmination of a three-year strategy designed to improve performance, ramp up its technology and significantly cut costs. By the end of 2026, the institution will have spent around £600m as part of a wider plan to reduce future costs. Barclays has stated that it has a further £300m or so worth of savings planned for the final three months of the year. Barclays Investment bank had a mixed third-quarter in which bond and currency trading fell by nearly 20pc year-on-year, to £1.01bn less than what was expected by the analysts. It was a better quarter in relation to equities revenue, which jumped 47pc and was the best result in this area since at least 2009. It is a challenging landscape for jobseekers and professionals currently as there have been several high-profile job cuts announced so far in October, including, 1,000 people from Ebay, 400 from EA and 1,000 employees from Netflix.
