The AI is incapable of caring if it So I only remembered a little bit of the virology of phages from what I learned in school--namely, that they infect bacteria and that they are everywhere. And when I saw some of the early comments posted here that were big on knee-jerk reactions and scarcasm but small on actual science, I decided to read the Wikipedia article. Phages don't infect eukaryotes; they only infect bacteria. And phage therapy has been successfully used to combat antibiotic resistance in humans, although the problem of resistance in general remains. This makes sense: bacteria replicate rapidly and have relatively little in the way of proofreading mechanisms, so when there is selection pressure, they mutate and develop resistance very easily, even to phages. So engineering a phage to target an antibiotic-resistant bacterial strain might work in the short term, but unless there's a way to cultivate phages that are able to adapt in response to (and not simply target) that strain, the strategy is intrinsically self-limiting. To those comments drawing ill-informed comparisons to engineering viruses that infect humans, know this: phages are literally everywhere on this planet. They are far, far more abundant than bacteria, and most people can appreciate how ubiquitous bacteria are. You already have phages inside of you, and in fact, they form an entire ecosystem. Same thing with bacteria--you have bacteria all over and inside your body. You have more bacterial cells coexisting on and inside you than you have your own cells. And what's more interesting is that these phages seem to be protective by helping to maintain a balanced microbiome. The concern I have with engineered phages is not that they would be harmful to the environment or to us, or that they could mutate to be harmful. That is extremely, extremely unlikely, because such a concern doesn't reflect an understanding of how phages actually function in real life and how they co-evolve with the bacteria they infect. If anything I would be concerned about spreading bacteria that become extensively resistant to antibiotics and phages. Thus we need better education about what phage therapy is, and to ensure that study participants are adequately informed of the potential risks and benefits. The direction such therapy would most likely need to take is quite similar to CRISPR and other gene therapies, in that it would need to be a form of personalized medicine. If we can develop computer models to help perform this personalization more efficiently, then I'm for it; such an approach may also prove useful for treating cancer. One other thing I forgot to mention. I'm skeptical that this "AI startup" actually did what it claimed to do, but since I have no evidence either way, I reserve my judgment until more details are given. If the startup can't or won't provide evidence, that in itself is suggestive of some kind of fraudulent behavior meant to hype up the company, a la Theranos/Elizabeth Holmes. But in so far as the underlying scientific principles are involved, the strategy seems plausible. I just don't know if this company ...Iran soon to create viruses that eat Americans? News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Is Making One More Play to Screw With the Midterms
2026-10-22

Trump Is Making One More Play to Screw With the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. With the midterm elections now just days away, it’s becoming clearer how the Trump administration plans to handle a potential blue wave that could destroy the president’s control of Congress for the remainder of his term. A key plank in this emerging strategy involves the most populous Democratic state in the country: California. The Department of Justice unveiled not one but two federal lawsuits over the past week that target the state’s elections, seeking the removal of countless voters from the rolls despite long-standing federal law that bans states from removing any voter within 90 days of a federal election. The trumped-up lawsuits make a showy attempt to go after a minuscule number of voters out of California’s whopping 23 million registered voters, but more importantly, the lawsuits conveniently lay the groundwork for a post-election legal fight—if things don’t go the president’s way. California being the Trump administration’s target should come as no surprise. Outgoing Gov. Gavin Newsom has been a constant critic of President Donald Trump. California is also the only Democrat-controlled state that successfully countered Trump’s mid-decade redistricting push, with voters there overwhelmingly passing a proposition to adopt new voting maps that could net up to five new Democrat-leaning House seats. The Trump administration unsuccessfully tried to convince the Supreme Court to ban California from using its new maps because they were “tainted by an unconstitutional racial gerrymander.” Clearly still salty about that matter, among others, the administration seems to have been cooking up a new way to attack California. Over the summer, Homeland Security sent California’s Secretary of State Shirley Weber a letter alleging that nearly 200,000 registered voters in California might be noncitizens (numerous secretaries of state received similar letters). But the letters included allegations alone, no evidence or data verifying DHS’s claims, which Weber said she flagged and asked to see, to no avail. Fast-forward to Oct. 13, when Weber, along with Los Angeles County Registrar-Recorder and County Clerk Dean Logan received another letter, this time from the DOJ—three weeks before Election Day—asking them to review a list of over 20,000 individuals suspected of being noncitizens who were sent a vote-by-mail ballot for the November election. DOJ wanted both Weber and Logan to direct election officials across the state to “segregate” submitted ballots from voters whose citizenship could not “immediately be verified,” refrain from sending ballots to any more people on its list of 20,000 suspected noncitizens “until we confirm their citizenship status,” and “if a ballot for any individual on the list has been returned, instruct the appropriate elections official to not verify the signature and/or tabulate the ballot until citizenship can be determined.” Weber and Logan were given approximately 48 hours to respond to the DOJ’s letter. And like clockwork, on Oct. 15 the DOJ filed a lawsuit asking a judge to issue a restraining order to prevent California election officials from processing ballots for any individual on its list of purported noncitizen voters. Never mind the fact that asking California to remove even a single name from its voter rolls at this point is a direct violation of the National Voter Registration Act, which established a 90-day “quiet period” before an election. This provision is meant to prevent states from doing exactly what the Trump administration is asking for: mass removals of voters too close to a federal election, which can cause confusion and prevent voters from fixing any registration issues with enough time left to cast their ballots. Weber called this out in responding to the DOJ’s Oct. 13 letter. She cited the NVRA and the unlawful ask being put on her and election officials across California. “With less than three weeks before the election, you are demanding unprecedented measures that threaten thousands of Californians’ right to vote, based on unverifiable information or methodology that you still fail to explain or provide,” she said. Weber also went after DOJ’s list of 20,000 purported noncitizens registered to vote in California, reiterating that she requested the underlying data and methodology months ago in order to attempt to verify the federal government’s claims. As it stands, the DOJ’s list effectively failed to “be of any investigative use.” On Tuesday, a Los Angeles County election official responded in court to the DOJ’s lawsuit. She called the DOJ’s demands potentially “catastrophic” and said they could end up halting all ballot counting, in person or by mail. She also revealed that election officials looked at a sample of 200 people on the federal government’s list and found that about 43 percent of them had a voter registration canceled, 71 percent had never actually cast a ballot, and nearly 40 percent indicated they were in fact U.S. citizens. This would not be the first time the Trump administration’s alleged noncitizen voting lists were found to be inaccurate. Last month, the Trump administration admitted that a list of nearly 16,000 purported noncitizen voters it flagged for Nevada election officials was wholly inaccurate. After Nevada Deputy Attorney General Greg D. Ott attempted to verify DHS’s claims, he found that every single person on DHS’s list was actually a lawful U.S. citizen. And since Trump retook the presidency, his DOJ has sued 30 states and D.C. to get access to their unredacted voter rolls. Twenty-six of those lawsuits were dismissed by federal judges, half of whom were appointed by Republican presidents, including by Trump himself. None of this matters to the Trump administration because its goal isn’t actually to weed out voter fraud, but to accomplish two things. First, these efforts seed doubt in America’s electoral process that could convince some voters to distrust the system to the point where they won’t bother casting a ballot. Second, these sham lawsuits give the administration a foothold in the courtroom in the event that a state’s election results are not satisfactory to Trump. Now, the DOJ and losing Republican candidates have a legal record to pull on to claim fraud and challenge state election results. Rick Hasen, law professor at UCLA and director of the Safeguarding Democracy Project, described this approach in a recent post on his Election Law Blog. Though Hasen expects the DOJ’s lawsuit against California “to go nowhere” in a court of law, he believes “this is a political document, potentially laying the groundwork for Trump to claim that the election results are illegitimate because California has not done enough to deal with noncitizen voting.” To put a finer point on this, the DOJ this week announced a second lawsuit against 28 Californians for election crimes. They’re each being accused of making false statements in their naturalization applications—those applications ask whether applicants previously registered to vote or claimed U.S. citizenship. A number of these folks, though, do not appear to have actually voted, and some registered to vote more than 20 years ago, likely by accident. “Twenty-eight out of 23 million registered voters in California is just … a drop in the bucket numerically,” California Attorney General Rob Bonta said. “It’s 0.0001 percent of all the registered voters, and it makes the point that there is no widespread voter fraud.” Research backs this up, with one study finding that when noncitizen voting and registration does happen, it’s usually due to bureaucratic error and not intentional. For instance, just this year New Jersey revealed that its Motor Vehicle Commission improperly registered about 6,600 noncitizens to vote back in 2023, but fewer than 400 of them actually cast ballots between 2023 and 2024. The Supreme Court has a hand in all of this, after it effectively emboldened the administration to pursue these types of lawsuits through an opinion issued just a month ago in DHS v. League of Women Voters. In a 6–3 decision, the justices have allowed the federal government to use an expanded federal database, known as the Systematic Alien Verification for Entitlements, to identify noncitizen voting in states’ voter rolls. What can be done now? Well, Americans are the only ones who carry the power of the vote, and right now they can control when they cast their ballots. As Joyce Vance, former U.S. attorney for the Northern District of Alabama, emphasized, “this is the time to vote early.” It will be far more difficult for the Trump administration to challenge election results with ballots submitted weeks ahead of Election Day, especially when races have a large margin of victory. And that’s only possible with strong voter turnout. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! In this week’s episode of Amicus, Dahlia Lithwick talks about the raid of Minnesota Rep. Ilhan Omar’s D.C. home with Andrew Weissmann, former chief of the Fraud Section at the Department of Justice. The Justice Department is attempting to build a denaturalization case against Omar, a particularly cruel new low for the president. Catch up on Slate’s “By the People” finale, in which Dahlia was joined by Stacey Abrams, Ilyse Hogue, and Daphna Renan for a live conversation about popular constitutionalism. They offered clear and concrete steps for reclaiming the U.S. Constitution, while also answering questions about court expansion and term limits. Slate is hosting another live event: Will the Midterms Be a Trick or a Treat? On Thursday, Oct. 29, our politics team will weigh whether a “blue tsunami” is actually coming, and whether it will have consequences for Trump, or whether all this talk is simply a phantom apparition. Register for this conversation to save your spot! Friend of Slate Madiba K. Dennie explains Salazar v. Paramount, an online privacy case the Supreme Court heard oral arguments for last week. It revolves around the Video Privacy Protection Act, and the court’s Republican-nominated justices seemed to suggest corporations should be insulated from legal liability when they intrude on consumers’ privacy for profit. The Supreme Court is considering how judges around the country handle criminal sentencing, and there is one justice who is particularly well equipped to hear this case: Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson. As Alexis Romero explains in Slate, she was previously the vice chair of the U.S. Sentencing Commission, but during oral arguments her fellow Republican-appointed justices seemed to disagree with her takes at every turn. The fourth installment of Frank Bowman’s “Try, Try Again?” series in Slate argues that President Trump can be impeached for authorizing the lethal boat strikes last year in the Caribbean and Eastern Pacific. U.S. experts have since concluded that the strikes were unlawful, while the United Nations also found the strikes to be illegal and that they could constitute “crimes against humanity.” Thank you for reading Executive Dysfunction! It’s a Blue State. It Was Ground Zero for ICE’s Brutality. It’s Somehow Also Democrats’ 2026 Weak Spot.
2026-10-22

It’s a Blue State. It Was Ground Zero for ICE’s Brutality. It’s Somehow Also Democrats’ 2026 Weak Spot.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. With less than two weeks to go before the midterms, the tale is the same as it has been all fall: It’s hard to find anything going Republicans’ way. Look around. While the GOP’s super PACs continue flooding Senate races in red states like Texas, Ohio, Kansas, and Iowa with ads, Senate Democrats’ super PAC is investing in Mississippi. In the House, Republican election groups are retreating from lost causes to defend core seats. But as Democrats put away House districts and Senate races in states like Georgia and North Carolina, one race is swimming curiously against the tide. On the same day that the election handicappers at the Cook Political Report shifted their rating of the Mississippi and South Carolina Senate races in Democrats’ favor, and fellow forecasters at Sabato’s Crystal Ball moved dozens of House races Democrats’ way, Cook made another change. It moved the Minnesota Senate race, a contest between Democratic Lt. Gov. Peggy Flanagan and Republican former sports reporter Michele Tafoya, from “Likely Democrat” to “Lean Democrat.” Crystal Ball had made a similar decision the previous week. In other words, forecasters believe Flanagan is still the favorite, but not by much. What’s going on here? Why, in a great environment for Democrats, and in a consistently blue state like Minnesota, is this race looking like a squeaker? It’s not the same as New Hampshire, Democrats’ other misbehaving light-blue state that should be a shoo-in this year but somehow isn’t. In the Granite State, Republicans are running a former senator from the state’s Republican royal family, and Republican outside groups invested substantially, and early, in that race. Senate Republicans’ main super PAC, meanwhile, hasn’t put any money behind Tafoya’s bid; each candidate’s campaign has spent a civilized couple-million dollars each. And it’s not that Minnesota is a runaway train trending redder demographically. It’s dominated by the Twin Cities, and has a high percentage of adults with college degrees, the dividing line on which so much of Trump-era political realignment hinges. Instead, Democrats’ inability to pull away in this race is a matter of candidate quality, and seismic news stories from Minnesota that garnered immense national attention. It’s not that Flanagan, an energetic progressive who harnessed anti-D.C. energy to win her primary over the more moderate Rep. Angie Craig, is a dunderhead. But Flanagan—like other progressive-wing nominees such as Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan and Troy Jackson in Maine—is clawing tooth and nail to get over the finish line even in this ideal Democratic environment while more moderate candidates like Roy Cooper in North Carolina and Jon Ossoff in Georgia have effectively put their races away. If the energy brought by progressive candidates is outweighing the softness they might have with the median voter, that’s not being picked up in polling. Tafoya, meanwhile, was mocked by many upon her entry to the race, as former Sunday Night Football sideline reporters don’t have substantial governing backgrounds. What former Sunday Night Football sideline reporters do have, though, is vast name recognition and an ability to speak well on TV. A lack of a substantial governing background has its advantages, too, as it allows her to draw up positions from scratch. She’s described herself as a “pro-choice Republican” (a posture that abortion rights advocates argue is not entirely on the level), has not had to take any votes on the Iran war, and has pledged to “stand up” to Trump when he’s wrong. What sets the race apart, aside from positions here or there, though, is that Tafoya, more than the average 2026 Republican in Trump’s America, has a lot of offensive material to work with. Flanagan is lieutenant to Gov. Tim Walz, who presided over a state welfare fraud scandal of such magnitude that it ended his bid for a third term. Tafoya has made this her central line of attack, arguing that Flanagan should be held accountable for the failures of the administration. “My opponent has been second in command in this state for eight years, drawing a taxpayer-funded paycheck,” Tafoya, in one of her many hits on Flanagan over the issue, said in a recent debate. “And what do we have to show for that? We have massive fraud, historic fraud. We have test scores down. We have crime spiking. We have a massive surplus turned into a deficit.” Flanagan’s defense, meanwhile, is that the social services agencies responsible for being defrauded were outside her portfolio, and that the blame rests with Walz. “The governor’s been very clear that the buck stops with him,” Flanagan said during the debate, positioning the governor under the bus. “He was the one who had oversight here.” Were that scandal to have been the last substantial national news story to explode out of Minnesota over the last year, Flanagan could be in even worse trouble. But it was the uncovering of those fraud scandals, along with its concentration of perpetrators within Minnesota’s Somali community, that egged the Trump administration into launching Operation Metro Surge, an assault on the Twin Cities from immigration enforcement authorities. That operation led to the deaths of two protesters under Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents’ heavy-handed tactics and turned the tide in both Minnesota and the nation against the administration’s mass deportation agenda. It’s the legacy of Operation Metro Surge that will make it so challenging for Tafoya to turn the race into a true toss-up before Election Day. Trump’s heavy-handedness is not just an abstraction for Minnesota Democrats. They’ve battled it in their streets. If Tafoya’s task of hammering the welfare scandal is simple, Flanagan’s task—bringing up Trump, and the need to stop him—is simpler. Earlier in the year, after she announced her candidacy, Tafoya said that she would be “honored” to have Trump’s endorsement, that she would “happily take” it. Trump has since become historically unpopular, and for a long time, she kept her distance. Just last week, though, Trump finally endorsed her, telling a crowd in Youngstown, Ohio (?), that “you have to vote for Michele Tafoya, who is a very respected person.” Trump said this with the briefest of pauses between “Michele” and “Tafoya,” as if he remembered in the moment that it might not be helpful to endorse her. Tafoya’s reaction to the endorsement was telling. “I heard about that,” she said when asked about it. “Look, this campaign is about bringing all Minnesotans together, whether they voted for President Trump or whether they voted for Kamala Harris.” She was similarly evasive when asked during the debate about whether she would appear with Trump should he campaign in Minnesota—something he has threatened to do—twice giving responses that weren’t even in the ballpark of answering the question. The RealClearPolitics polling average has Flanagan up 2 points over Tafoya at the moment, while an internal poll released by Senate Democrats’ super PAC, the Senate Majority PAC, at the beginning of the month showed her up by 8 points. Whatever it is, the same forecasters who are moving the race in Republicans’ direction “still believe the national environment will lift Flanagan to victory,” as the Cook Political Report’s Jessica Taylor put it. It’s possible that the polls are way off, that Minnesota will fall in line with a result closer to what you’d expect, and that Flanagan wins in a walk. It’s just strange that we don’t know, particularly when, in much swingier states, Democrats have already seemingly clinched victory. If the race is that tight, though, it’s a reminder that no “wave” election is ever uniform. Candidates matter, and state-specific issues matter. Weird things happen. In 2014, in an environment where Republicans won their largest House majority since the 1920s while picking up governor’s races in Maryland, Illinois, and Massachusetts, Democrats knocked off the incumbent GOP governor of Pennsylvania by 10 points. In 2018, when Democrats picked up 40 seats in the House, they lost a Florida Senate seat (and governor’s race) before the state had fully calcified into its present redness. There’s a not-unrealistic scenario in which Democrats win the Texas and Minnesota Senate seats by the same margin—or, if the polls are underestimating Republicans, lose them by the same margin. A Republican-on-Republican Fight Is Consuming the Midterms
2026-10-21

A Republican-on-Republican Fight Is Consuming the Midterms

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. With T-minus 13 days until the midterms, polls suggest Republicans are headed for a wipeout. So what is Donald Trump—a self-proclaimed “frickin’ genius”—up to? Escalating a fight with his own party over his support for Russia and its leader, Vladimir Putin. Beyond the life-or-death stakes for Ukrainians, the spat has forced many Republican officials to spend precious time criticizing the administration instead of Democrats. But the bigger story is that Trump’s long-standing affinity for Russia may finally be coming back to bite him in a meaningful way. Back up: Last time you wrote about this, Trump was buying Russian diesel and criticizing Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky. What’s happened since? A lot. Last week, Trump signed an executive order immunizing Russia from new sanctions under a law Congress passed to punish Putin’s aggression. On Monday, the Washington Post revealed that the administration is holding up interceptor missiles Ukraine needs to defend against Russian attacks. On Tuesday, the Wall Street Journal reported that Elon Musk may have conspired with Trump to throttle Ukraine’s access to SpaceX-made battlefield satellites. (“Some fools still think the legacy news is real. Amazing,” Musk wrote today on X in what read like a classic nondenial denial.) And on Wednesday, Steve Witkoff, the president’s real-estate buddy turned globetrotting negotiator, visited Russia and praised Putin, an autocrat who invaded a neighboring country and has reportedly ordered the assassinations of domestic political opponents, for his humanity. “You talk so much about my president, President Trump, being a humanitarian,” Witkoff said. “You are a humanitarian also, sir, and I salute that.” Not well! “WTF, Steve?” Sen. Thom Tillis of North Carolina posted on Wednesday. “Another embarrassment,” wrote Rep. Don Bacon of Nebraska. While campaigning in Maine, Sen. Susan Collins said Trump should reverse his decision to waive sanctions on Russia. It’s true that those lawmakers are frequent Trump critics, but the backlash is starting to break containment. Rep. Max Miller of Ohio, a former Trump aide who has likened himself to the president as he fights domestic abuse allegations, labeled Witkoff’s bootlicking “ridiculous.” Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick of Pennsylvania called for an investigation into whether Musk slowed Ukraine’s satellite access, calling it “likely a violation of the law.” Putin “is not our friend, and he is certainly no humanitarian,” wrote Sen. Joni Ernst of Iowa. “His relentless attacks on innocent civilians in Ukraine show his total disregard for human life.” As the Wall Street Journal’s conservative editorial board thundered on Sunday, “When the election is over there needs to be a bipartisan reckoning for Mr. Trump’s budding alliance with the Kremlin against Ukraine, Europe and American interests.” Speaking of the election: Neither Trump nor his party would seem to benefit from a public spat. So why are they doing this? To some extent, Trump may think he’s helping. He blames Ukrainian attacks on Russian oil refineries for the high cost of diesel. It’s actually Trump’s war in Iran that caused the spike, but importing Russian fuel could help save vulnerable GOP candidates if it eases prices even a little. Witkoff’s praise also came after Putin agreed to release a 74-year-old American whom Russia had detained for years. Meanwhile, Collins, Fitzpatrick, and other Republicans in tough races may earnestly care about Ukraine. But they also stand to benefit politically from shivving a president whose approval ratings keep setting new lows. OK, but if they really wanted to stop him, couldn’t Trump’s Republican critics in Congress … you know, do something? Yes. Worse, Congress has enabled some of Trump’s steps to betray Ukraine. The sanctions law he sidestepped—named for the late Sen. Lindsey Graham, a longtime Russia foe—contained language that let Trump exempt Russia. As a result, some Democrats had refused to support the bill. A bipartisan majority of Congress, most of them Republicans, passed it anyway. Trump has gotten away with cozying up to Putin for a decade now. So should we really expect his party to pay a price now? It’s fair to wonder. During the 2016 campaign, Hillary Clinton memorably argued that Trump would become Putin’s “puppet.” Enough voters overlooked those warnings to give Trump the White House and Republicans unified control of Congress. In 2024, Kamala Harris predicted that Trump would “give up” Ukraine. Again, Trump and his party won anyway. But this time really could be different. Polls suggest Republicans are struggling to motivate their voters, and an election-eve GOP-on-GOP fight probably doesn’t help. The president has also taken the less popular side. Americans as a whole support Ukraine less uniformly than they did when the war began, but more Republicans than not still like Zelensky more than Putin, favor aiding Ukraine, and back sanctions against Russia. Faced with backlash, Trump could try to change course; as we’ve explained, he has repeatedly flip-flopped on Ukraine. But with millions of Americans already casting early ballots, any turnabout might come too late to make a political difference. Before we transition to some happier reads, an update on yesterday’s newsletter: After Vivek Ramaswamy’s campaign twice declined to tell us whether it was behind an ad featuring his gubernatorial opponent’s estranged mother, the Ohio Republican has admitted that his campaign made the ad—and plans to spend $4 million airing it statewide. So as ugly as that race looked yesterday, it could get uglier still. OK, time to pivot. If you’re in search for some evening enjoyment, my colleagues think you should: Mark your calendar for a special Slate event: This spooky season, the midterm elections are full of frights, but Slate’s politics team is here to warn you about any jumpscares. Join senior political reporter Jim Newell, politics (and Slatest!) editor Patrick Reis, and Mary Harris and Lizzie O’Leary of What Next and What Next: TBD for a live Zoom event on Thursday, Oct. 29, to discuss what’s real, what’s noise, and what to expect next. See you there! Tune into a sharp new TV show: The creators of The Good Wife and The Good Fight have spent years skewering Big Tech. Their new series, Cupertino, is laser-focused on how Silicon Valley is undermining America’s laws and values. Slate’s Sam Adams talked with creative duo Robert and Michelle King about how they made exactly the right show for our current moment. Peer into one of social media’s dark corners: Family estrangement is one of the most complicated situations to navigate for parents and children alike. When writer Christina Wyman discovered that her mother was active in a Facebook group for estranged parents, she found herself in a toxic echo chamber that was doing more harm than good. Listen to her story on the latest episode of ICYMI. Give your brain a workout: Who is a “philosopher associated with deontology and zero-effort puns” (four letters)? Who “played Ahnold’s twin in Twins” (five letters)? Solve these clues in today’s crossword. Congrats! You’re more than halfway through the week. Enjoy the night and see you tomorrow. The Trump Administration Is Promoting the “Good Guy With a Gun” Lie at Just the Right Time
2026-10-21

The Trump Administration Is Promoting the “Good Guy With a Gun” Lie at Just the Right Time

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. On Thursday, a 16-year-old armed with a pump-action 12-gauge shotgun that his mother allegedly bought him for his birthday and a backpack full of ammunition opened fire at Silver Rail Elementary School, in Bend, Oregon, killing three children and a teacher. A 7-year-old student and another teacher were critically injured. The tragedy could have been worse if not for the heroic actions of an unarmed teacher who threw her cellphone at the gunman before tackling him, and a father of two students at the school, who heard the commotion and used his pistol to kill the shooter. While both people who intervened to stop the shooter deserve high praise for their selfless heroism, some commentators—including President Donald Trump and the National Rifle Association—are celebrating the “good guy with a gun” while ignoring the unarmed teacher who intervened first. This “good guy with a gun” myth is a seductive marketing tool for the gun industry. Many find comfort in the promise that the solution to our most urgent insecurities is readily available at the nearest gun shop. If we are afraid, we need only purchase a firearm to ensure we are ready when a “bad guy” breaches our cherished boundaries. But there are critical problems with the armed “good guy” trope. One is that the most reliable empirical research on defensive gun use demonstrates that incidents in which a “good guy with a gun” stops a “bad guy” are vanishingly rare. The overwhelming majority of fact-based, peer-reviewed research shows definitively that more guns are associated with more gun-related deaths and injuries. Consistent scientific evidence demonstrates that the net effect of greater intrusion of guns into public life increases homicide, so any benefits from the relatively rare cases of defensive gun use can’t alter the calculus that more innocent lives will be lost as guns become easier to acquire and carry. Another problem is that our cultural assumptions about “good guys” are laden with biases, particularly racist assumptions that undermine the safety and self-defensive rights of people of color. We have witnessed the exclusionary effects of the “good guy” narrative when law-abiding Black gun owners, like Philando Castile, are shot and killed by police who see them as a threat in spite of their lawful comportment. Armed Black “good guys,” like security guard Jemel Roberson and U.S. Army veteran E.J. Bradford—who responded to public shootings in November 2018 with armed heroism—have been shot and killed by police, who misidentified them as armed “bad guys.” Similarly, states with so-called “stand your ground” laws, enforce them in ways that disproportionately favor leniency toward white people who have killed Black people. Women who “stand their ground” against men they know, meanwhile, are more likely to be punished than to be exonerated or celebrated as “good guys.” “Stand your ground” laws have spread to a significant majority of the states in spite of these biases and studies demonstrating their connection to increased rates of firearm homicide. Despite data showing that true incidents of self-defensive gun use are rare, the “gun rights” lobby has continued to promote the “good guy with a gun” narrative in the service of deregulatory firearm policies. Recently, it succeeded in persuading the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention to post faulty and misleading data on the agency’s website. The site currently presents a vastly overblown estimate of 500,000 to 2.5 million defensive gun-use incidents annually. These figures are based on suspect “research” by a small handful of people whose work has been soundly discredited. Much of this research depends on surveys, which are inaccurate metrics for determining whether someone was actually defending themself when they pulled a gun on another person. Reliable scholarship—including a 1997 study by Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health scholar David Hemenway—reveals a significant confirmation bias in such surveys: A person who pulls their gun is more likely to depict their actions in a positive and even heroic light. Even though multiple reliable studies show that self-reported defensive gun-use incidents are significantly exaggerated, the propaganda of armed self-defense remains influential, informing policies that continue to deregulate firearm ownership and carry. Twenty-nine states allow people to carry firearms into public spaces without a license or training, and approximately two-thirds of the states have adopted some version of a “stand your ground” law. There are deadly consequences to the mass distribution of this misinformation. The most respected peer-reviewed data show that guns are not reliable tools of self-defense. To the contrary, guns in the home significantly raise the risk of intimate partner homicide, and guns are the leading cause of death for American children. Instead of protecting ourselves and each other, Americans are using guns to kill each other, and—more frequently—to kill ourselves. While we await the details of the final struggle to end the bloodshed at Silver Rail Elementary School, the fact remains that three young children and a teacher were murdered. “It could have been worse” is cold comfort to the grieving families, the school, and community members whose lives and sense of safety are shattered. We might also reflect on the senselessness of the incident itself, the tragedy of recurring violence that might have been prevented if firearms were a little less readily available. According to the K–12 School Shooting Database, 2025 saw 235 shooting incidents at K–12 schools across the U.S. In 2026, we have witnessed approximately 150 school shootings thus far. Perhaps, instead of promoting more guns as the solution to this ongoing nightmare, we might focus on prevention. The data reveal a very clear picture: Over the past 20 years, the 10 states with the highest levels of gun regulation enjoyed a substantial 45.3 percent drop in the murder rate, while the 20 states with the lowest levels of gun regulation showed only an 11.9 percent decline. The Trump administration is merely exacerbating the “good guy with a gun” myth with the CDC’s new database. When our nation’s primary source for public health information has been captured by the purveyors of “gun rights,” we have capitulated to a defeatist fantasy of armed safety, ultimately placing profit for the firearms industry over public health. www.theregister.com - Articles
Nvidia's Huang: Don't mention the AI-pocalypse
2026-10-22

Nvidia's Huang: Don't mention the AI-pocalypse

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization Jensen Huang wants the world to stop fretting about AI and start building with it. At Nvidia's GTC event in Berlin, he dismissed predictions of human extinction, defended AI's job prospects, and pitched a $4,000 computer as the family robot. The Nvidia chief's relentless optimism occasionally gave way to irritation during a question-and-answer session. "If we don't describe a future worthy to be part of, then we're not going to lead people there," he said. "We have to describe a future worthy of creating. We have to describe a future not of fear, but of optimism. Who wants to create a future that's worse than today's? We don't have to work hard to create a future worse than today. We just let it happen, isn't that right?" Huang said that we still have to ask the hard questions about safety and jobs, but "it's important, of course, that we don't make it up. It is not helpful in describing an optimistic future when you say that it will be the end of the human civilization. First of all, it's not true. It's made up. I know it gets headlines," he said, dismissing such stories as clickbait. When asked if AI really makes people more productive, he explained that there are two ways to think about that: either it helps you do the same old thing more efficiently, or it helps you do things you couldn't before. "Most of engineering, most of design, has to do with exploring options. Try, experiment, try and experiment, iterate, experiment, iterate," he said. "What's the best combination? And you can only have so much time to try different versions. Now I have AI to go off and explore that universe. That is productivity. And inside our company, we do this every single day now. We're exploring the universe of design options that are within the constraints that we care about, that are physical or realistic constraints." Huang pointed to construction and infrastructure work generated by the AI datacenter boom. "Construction workers, plumbers, electricians, networking engineers, all of those incredible jobs are being created in the United States. I think it already has created a million jobs, and then the layer above that, look at all the startups that are being created," he claimed. "That's productivity, increasing the GDP, increasing our growth, which also results in increasing how busy we are. But that's the end. AI makes jobs, and hopefully doesn't take jobs." Asked about people already losing work to AI - global financial services group Barclays Bank said only this morning it is chopping 5,000 workers and relying more on AI -  Huang argued that technological change has always displaced some jobs while creating others. "Now, with AI, of course, some jobs are not necessary as much as before, or maybe not as interesting as before. And some new jobs and new companies and new businesses are necessary. It's the nature of the world," he stated. On safety, Huang pointed to Nvidia's OpenShell sandbox technology, designed to restrict agents to the resources needed for a task. "If you're making an omelette, you don't need a hammer," he said. But he got hot under the collar when asked about slowing down the pace of AI development while the risks were evaluated, in light of incidents such as AI agents from Anthropic and OpenAI breaking into other computer systems. "You would favor pacing the frontier? I favor them doing whatever they need to do to not bring harm to society," Huang said. "You, sir, if you believe that you should not leave your house because you're inappropriately dressed, or somehow you're going to bring harm to society, could you please not come out?" he told the questioner. "The message from the labs is that there's a collective action problem, and that some minimum standards in coordination would be good here. That's a different question. If you know something is already delivering harm, you got to stop running," he added. Nvidia has a commercial interest in keeping the AI build-out moving. It reported $215.9 billion in revenue for fiscal 2026 and $120 billion in net profit. In the first half of its fiscal 2027, those net profits were already close to the prior year's . The opportunity isn't limited to just chips either. Huang put the price of licensing Nvidia's software stack at about $2,000 per GPU annually. The CEO's domestic vision was also on show. Asked when households could run large AI models, he held up a DGX Spark system. "This computer has a chip called N1X inside, and N1X has 128 gigabytes of memory… It's $4,000. You're right. It's quite expensive. However, that computer was designed to sit on the network in your house, and it would have agents that are used by all of your family members. So it's kind of like a TV. It's sitting in your house. It's shared by everyone, and everybody's agents will be running on DGX Spark," he explained. As an example, Huang said his home agent had researched Berlin's best schnitzel and produced a brochure with restaurant pictures and menus. "So that agent, you know, that computer will become essentially your family's robot. It has no arms and legs, but it's an agent, so it's kind of like a robot. And this one is just for your home. So that's kind of how I see the future. This is pretty affordable. This is a personal computer." Affordable if your net worth is around $180-$190 billion. Maybe not to the average household struggling to pay bills in a cost of living crisis. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Hackers pile on PHP2Shell as AWS reveals attacks began before disclosure
2026-10-22

Hackers pile on PHP2Shell as AWS reveals attacks began before disclosure

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed Attackers are hammering servers with millions of attempts to exploit the critical PHP2Shell vulnerabilities, with Amazon revealing that someone was already going after the flaws four days before they were publicly disclosed. Cloudflare says it has recorded a staggering increase in attacks targeting the two vulnerabilities, tracked as CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, since details began circulating earlier this week. According to Cloudflare CEO Matthew Prince, the company spotted thousands of exploitation attempts in the 10 hours before the vulnerabilities were publicly announced. That figure jumped to tens of thousands within 45 minutes of disclosure, hundreds of thousands within two hours, and has since climbed into the millions. And the attackers aren't simply recycling the same exploit. Cloudflare said it has seen miscreants tweaking their payloads in an attempt to sneak past its web application firewall (WAF) protections, apparently using feedback from blocked requests to work out what might get through. Security outfit Hadrian also sounded the alarm on October 21, reporting that PHP2Shell was being "exploited in the wild at scale." "Most of the attacks we observe abuse PHP's session upload-progress path, and we expect more exploit paths to appear before and after the fix ships," the company warned. More worrying, some attackers appear to have started before the vulnerabilities were even public knowledge. Amazon Threat Intelligence says it detected an attempt to exploit PHP2Shell on October 17, four days before patches were released. The activity was picked up by MadPot, Amazon's network of honeypots designed to lure attackers and record what they get up to. "While the vulnerability was under embargo, the targeting of a honeypot from an anonymized IP address suggests the activity was conducted by a threat actor, though it remains unclear how the actor acquired exploit information prior to disclosure," Amazon said. The retail and cloud giant said it detected multiple proof-of-concept exploits on October 21, with the earliest appearing around 0200 UTC, some 12 hours before the patches were released. Over the following six hours, further attempts arrived from different anonymized infrastructure, using a variety of payloads. Amazon reckons this points to multiple attackers rather than a single group. Whether any of those attempts would have worked against real-world systems remains unclear. The findings add another layer to an already messy disclosure. As The Register reported on Wednesday, the PHP Foundation has accused supply-chain security vendor Chainguard of putting websites at risk by publishing patched PHP builds before the agreed disclosure deadline. However, Amazon's October 17 detection suggests at least one cybercrim was already trying to exploit the bugs days before that incident. The vulnerabilities themselves are particularly nasty. Both carry a critical 9.2 severity rating and affect PHP's handling of memory, potentially allowing unauthenticated attackers to execute malicious code on vulnerable servers. Unlike flaws in individual plugins or applications, these bugs affect the PHP runtime itself, putting a potentially enormous number of websites at risk regardless of which content management system they use. Cloudflare says it has deployed WAF protections across its free and paid plans, although it has stressed that these are no substitute for patching. With attackers already trying their luck before the fixes were available, admins may have more than just a patching backlog to worry about. Amazon is advising organizations to investigate suspicious activity dating back to at least October 17, rather than assuming their systems were safe until the vulnerabilities became public. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Microsoft says ConnectWise breach let Chinese spies loose on 2,250 cloud tenants
2026-10-22

Microsoft says ConnectWise breach let Chinese spies loose on 2,250 cloud tenants

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
AI agents did the dirty work, with hackers gaining privileged access in some environments in under three hours China-aligned attackers used AI agents and compromised ConnectWise application credentials to target more than 2,250 Microsoft 365 tenants, Microsoft says. The Windows giant revealed this week that the attackers, tracked as Storm-0940, used an open source AI penetration-testing tool called ARTEX to automate attacks against Microsoft 365 environments. In some cases, they went from picking a target to gaining privileged cloud access in less than three hours. The findings shed more light on the ConnectWise security incident, which saw the IT management vendor pull the plug on its Cloud Backup, SaaS Security, and Cloud Management services after discovering unauthorized access to application identities. ConnectWise didn't initially identify who was responsible, but Microsoft has now pointed the finger at Storm-0940, a China-linked espionage crew that's been operating since at least 2021. The group has traditionally relied on the usual tricks, including password spraying and exploiting internet-facing applications, but this time it used AI to do some of the heavy lifting. ARTEX is an open source penetration-testing framework written in Go that uses AI agents to automate everything from reconnaissance and vulnerability discovery to credential attacks and data theft. Microsoft says Storm-0940 began using it as early as September to target organizations worldwide, including government agencies, defense contractors, banks, manufacturers, and tech firms. Between October 10 and 14, the hackers made more than 151,000 failed sign-in attempts against 327 accounts at eight organizations. Microsoft observed no successful logins from that password-spraying campaign, but then the attackers shifted to compromised ConnectWise application identities. The compromised application credentials let the attackers exploit permissions customers had already granted to ConnectWise services, without needing an interactive user login. Armed with ARTEX, Storm-0940 attempted to authenticate against more than 2,250 Microsoft 365 tenants, at times reaching 2,900 attempts a minute. Microsoft also observed AI-accelerated activity through Microsoft Graph, including reconnaissance, application credential changes, and attempts to modify privileges, potentially allowing the attackers to stick around even after the original credentials were revoked. Most attempts failed, though Microsoft says the hackers still managed to establish persistent privileged access at numerous organizations. It hasn't disclosed how many customers were compromised or whether any data was stolen, cautioning that a successful login doesn't necessarily mean information was accessed. ConnectWise CEO Manny Rivelo said the company had contained the affected application connections and was gradually restoring services, although shutting down those connections wouldn't necessarily boot out attackers who had already established a foothold in customer environments. ConnectWise has been here before. In May 2025, it admitted that a "sophisticated nation-state actor" had broken into its systems and compromised a small number of customers using its ScreenConnect remote access software. There's nothing to suggest the two attacks are linked. Nor is Storm-0940 alone in making use of ARTEX. Microsoft says it has spotted attackers from several geographic regions using the framework, which is freely available for penetration testing. The underlying techniques are nothing new – stolen credentials, password spraying, and application abuse – but Microsoft says AI helped Storm-0940 execute them faster, with some attacks progressing from initial targeting to privileged cloud access in under three hours. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Veteran ESA probe rolls out welcome mat for Japan's Phobos mission
2026-10-22

Veteran ESA probe rolls out welcome mat for Japan's Phobos mission

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Mars Express could support MMX's 2027 arrival as another 'last ever' extension keeps old spacecraft in flight Japan's mission to the Martian moon Phobos launched this week, with a veteran European probe expected to be waiting when it arrives. JAXA, Japan's space agency, confirmed that the Martian Moons eXploration (MMX) spacecraft launched aboard an H3 rocket on October 19 at 19:41 UTC – October 20 in Japan. On October 21, it said that initial critical operations, including solar array deployment, were complete. Equipment checks will follow as the spacecraft prepares for its outbound cruise. The probe is due to reach Mars in 2027, deploy the Idefix rover on Phobos, and attempt one or two landings on the moon itself. The rover is named after the dog from the Asterix comics. Scientists plan to collect about 10 g of material from Phobos and send it to Earth, with a return planned for 2031. The European Space Agency (ESA) is supporting the trip with Mars Express (MEX), which has orbited Mars since 2003. A former team member told The Register that plans include joint observations and imaging of MMX's landing site. MEX's latest mission extension runs to 2029, covering MMX's expected arrival. The former team member described it as definitely the last – before adding that "the first 'last ever extension' was in about 2007." Discussions about another "last ever extension" could begin in 2028 or 2029, with support for MMX and the Rosalind Franklin rover among the potential reasons to keep MEX going. Funding and scientific returns would determine the outcome. A software update announced in May 2025 reduced wear on its gyroscopes, potentially allowing them to last until 2034. Rosalind Franklin is due for launch in 2028 after repeated delays and Russia's ejection from the project. MEX will likely be able to image the rover's landing site on the surface of Mars and take part in data relay operations, though ESA's Trace Gas Orbiter (TGO) will be the main relay asset. Several years ago, another team member joked that MEX was so tough it would punch straight through Phobos if sent there for disposal. We wouldn't take that as an engineering assessment, but it says something about the team's affection for their spacecraft. MEX's longevity is a tribute to its builders and operators. With funding through 2029, it could welcome MMX next year. Further extensions might keep it working through the sample-return departure and Rosalind Franklin's arrival. "We all want our '30 years in flight' T-shirts," the former team member said. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... IT department and auditors at major pharmacy chain got into a rip-roaring fight about using 'password' on Cisco firewall
2026-10-22

IT department and auditors at major pharmacy chain got into a rip-roaring fight about using 'password' on Cisco firewall

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Misaligned incentives led to an intractable argument Welcome back to PWNED, the column where we talk about companies and organizations that leave the doors wide open to attackers. This week’s sad story involves a major American pharmacy chain that couldn’t fill a prescription for password security. Have a story about someone leaving a gaping hole in their network? Share it with us at pwned@sitpub.com. Anonymity is available upon request. Our tale comes courtesy of red team and pen testing specialist Dahvid Schloss, who currently serves as COO of Suzu Labs, a cybersecurity company that boasts “we break your systems before adversaries do.” Over a career spanning more than 15 years, Schloss has assessed the security of numerous businesses, including a large US pharmacy retailer that he asked us not to name. He wasn’t asked to break in, just to do an audit on all of the org’s corporate switches, routers, and firewalls. “So, I come across this firewall,” Schloss said. “That is an edge firewall, meaning this is what touches the internet. This part will add its enable password, which is the term Cisco uses for administrator. It had the enable password as 'password.' Just p-a-s-s-w-o-r-d.” As Schloss noted, the enable command on Cisco devices allows the user to gain administrative rights to the firewall, controlling network traffic. If someone connected to that firewall, either via SSH or through an exploit, and got normal user privileges, they could then elevate those privileges to admin and do something like shut off all traffic from the intranet to the outside world. Schloss actually didn’t guess that the password was “password,” but instead was able to decrypt the file holding the passwords by breaking Cisco’s own protection, something he said is easy to do. “That enable password was stored as Cisco IOS type 7, which isn’t really a hash,” he said. “It’s more of a reversible encoding with a publicly known key. Even back then, it was best practice not to use it.” But even if the password were stored in a secure place, it would be very easy for a threat actor to guess. So he marked the password failure down as a “high find,” a bad type of vuln that needs to be fixed right away. When he brought the password to the company’s auditing department, they put him on a phone call with the auditing team, the entire IT team, his own manager, and others. On the packed call, the auditing team, who had commissioned Schloss’s work, was very concerned about the firewall password, but the IT team didn’t think it was a very serious problem. The two sides screamed at each other for more than an hour while Schloss and his colleagues sat there quietly, staring at their cameras. “The audit team is like ‘yeah, we gotta fix this.’ And the directors were really harping on ‘this needs to be done’ and the IT team starts firing back,” Schloss explained. “It’s like ‘Why? Who’s gonna? What does it matter? No, this isn’t a high-priority find. This is maybe a medium. Yeah, it’s bad, but it’s not that bad.’” The call dragged on so long that Schloss was unable to finish presenting his findings and recommendations. The conflict was so severe that the client continued to follow up and ask him to explain what he found again for the next three years – long after he had moved on to other projects. He later learned that the auditing department controlled the bonuses for the IT team and there was a stipulation that any “high finding” would eliminate those bonuses. So they weren’t just arguing about “password” - they were really talking about the compensation for an entire division. The lesson here is more than “don’t use the word ‘password’ as a password.” It's also about misaligned incentives. Tying security posture to compensation seems like a good way to incentivize workers to follow best practices, but it also leads them to deny responsibility and minimize mistakes when they're found. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... But why oh why didn't they call Claude clone Fauxtoshop? UK government hasn't the foggiest how much it's spending on digital transformation
2026-10-22

UK government hasn't the foggiest how much it's spending on digital transformation

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Years of promises, but officials unable to put a figure on investment The UK government says its finance systems cannot produce consistent annual figures showing how much departments and public bodies spend on digital transformation. The Science and Technology Committee's June report warned that spending was fragmented and the government lacked a clear plan for a digital state. The UK public sector spends more than £26 billion annually on digital technology, but that broad estimate does not provide a comparable departmental breakdown. Responding to the committee, the government agreed with the "intent to publish annual data on digital and data-driven spending" but said it did not "believe this is currently practicable." Existing finance systems could not "provide consistent, reliable and comparable data across departments." "The government is open to this as a direction of travel and will work with HM Treasury to consider how this could be developed over time," the response said. Committee chair Dame Chi Onwurah, who is due to address Parliament on Thirsday, said: "There's no shortage of hype about how technology can improve public services and reduce costs. Yet, frustratingly, the government cannot provide a clear picture of how much is being spent on digital transformation across departments and public bodies. "At a time when ministers are asking taxpayers to fund significant investment in technology, it's concerning that they cannot show where the money is going or how value for money is being measured. This must be addressed as a matter of urgency." The government abolished the Department for Science, Innovation and Technology in the summer, dividing its responsibilities between the Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport, the Cabinet Office, and the newly created Department for Business, Innovation, Science and Trade. Onwurah said: "Additionally, the abolition of DSIT has only highlighted concerns about who is responsible for driving digital transformation across government. I call on the government to revisit its response and create clear leadership that can drive and deliver the scale of change needed." The committee also called for the government to end Palantir's involvement in the NHS Federated Data Platform (FDP), citing concerns over sovereignty and vendor lock-in. The relationship has drawn criticism over £60 million in earlier contracts awarded without competition. The government said it would review the FDP contract "in line with standard contract management processes to inform a decision on whether to exercise the optional two-year extension." The response did not commit to an early exit. It said any termination would have to comply with the contract's terms and public procurement law. "If a decision is taken to not extend Palantir's contract, then the contract includes controls to support transition and continuity of services, ensuring that operational delivery and patient services are protected," the response said. Onwurah said the committee was pleased the government had committed to developing a strategy to tackle vendor lock-in and build greater internal capacity. "Ministers should now go further by exercising the break clause in its FDP contract and take steps to reduce our dependence on a small number of large US tech firms," she added. Palantir maintains that its technology provides value for money and helps the NHS improve efficiency. ® For those who find taking a phone out of their pocket just so last century GPU chief doesn't want to hear about job losses or the end of human civilization PARTNER CONTENT: AI is driving liquid cooling adoption, but hybrid architectures will help operators balance density, flexibility, and operational realities Cloudflare reports millions of exploit attempts, while Amazon spotted attackers trying their luck four days before patches landed The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... The Verge
Bill Gates says to prepare for 20 years of ‘tumultuous’ AI disruption
2026-10-22

Bill Gates says to prepare for 20 years of ‘tumultuous’ AI disruption

The Verge | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
The Microsoft cofounder isn’t an AI doomer, but he says it’s still “the most dangerous weapon of all time.” Today, I’m talking to Bill Gates, cofounder and former CEO of Microsoft and chairman of the Gates Foundation. You may have noticed Bill in the news more lately — he’s been on something of a tour for the past few months, ever since publishing an essay in late August titled “The turbulent AI era is here. The choices we make now are critical.” In his piece, Gates ratcheted up his rhetoric around AI safety, writing that “even under the best circumstances, the transition to this new AI era will be one of the most turbulent times in human history.” In interviews since, Gates has called AI “the most dangerous thing that humans have ever gone near,” and that AI “makes nuclear weapons look like nothing.” It is clear he does not think the current policy response from the White House, or the lack of coordinated efforts from the AI industry itself, is sufficient to combat this threat. Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here. So I wanted to know: Is Bill Gates an AI doomer? I also had a series of questions downstream from what he’s been arguing about AI. How can we ever hope to control AI, and align it to humanity, if we can’t even agree on what it is and whether it’s conscious? How would you even technically construct a solution to alignment that could be enforced, not just for closed-source models but open-source ones, too? Bill gave us a big chunk of his time to dig into these issues with him, and the resulting conversation is a really illuminating one. He thinks we need to prepare for roughly two decades of AI disruption, and he says this period could be so painful it might split society in two — between people comfortable living near the technological frontier, and those who reject it. We also talked about the labor considerations, the nihilism of younger generations toward technological progress, and what should make us hopeful about the future. This is a deep one. Okay: Bill Gates, the cofounder and former CEO of Microsoft. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Bill Gates, you are the co-founder and former CEO of Microsoft. You’re also the chairman of the Gates Foundation. Welcome to Decoder. It’s good to talk to you again. You and I have spoken many times over the years. I would say that the circumstances of this conversation feel very different. Previously, when you and I would talk, you were always very hopeful about technology and what it could bring. You had a Netflix series a few years ago. You talked to The Verge, and I want to just read you this quote. You said, “I believe AI is a beneficial thing, we just need to shape it in the right way.” I would say you still agree with that, you just think the time to shape it is now. Yeah, I agree with that. I’d say the response broadly hasn’t been adequate to the speed that the challenge is arising. I’m very concerned that the bad stuff will not be minimized and that a lot of good stuff could come fairly slowly if we don’t manage it the right way. I’m very concerned that the bad stuff will not be minimized and that a lot of good stuff could come fairly slowly if we don’t manage it the right way I want to have this conversation with you in three parts. I want to understand how you feel about AI as a technology today and why it’s advancing so quickly. I’m very curious about the technology, and the technical solutions to potentially control it. There are policy solutions as well, but it seems like we have to build an awful lot of technology. I’m also very curious to talk about who might impose those solutions and controls, because that seems very much up for debate right now as well. So let’s just start at the start. What has made you feel the urgency that AI is arriving at a speed that means we’re not ready to control it? The progress over the last year has been really quite amazing, starting with the ability to write code and find cyber flaws, the ability to reason in biology, the ability to form relationships with kids or other people, the ability to disrupt the job market. The incredible power that exists today means that every one of those things will happen and society needs to be ready. Do you see that happening as a technology as a whole or in the products that are instantiated by the companies that are making AI today? What I mean is that AI is a broad set of models that exist in the world, some open source, some proprietary. Then there are the companies that make these products and there are the leaders of those companies that make these products. At every level, there’s a different relationship you or I might have to AI. Are you seeing these risks and these capabilities broadly in the field, or in the particular companies led by the particular people? The risk isn’t one model versus another model. Certainly, the near-term danger of models where you can remove all the safeguards is very high and quite different from models where you monitor to make sure that cyberattackers and bioterrorists are not able to exploit the capability of the model to do gigantic harm. The big near-term risk really comes from models without safeguards. On a longer timeframe, the control issue relates to any state-of-the-art model, but it’s always easier when you can make sure safeguards are maintained. The reason I’m asking that question that way is maybe you don’t trust Sam Altman today, but you trust Dario Amodei. Or maybe you don’t trust either one of those and you trust Satya Nadella at Microsoft, and that is a market dynamic where maybe we’re all going to pick the safest model or the safest company, and you think that is diffuse. You think eventually the technology will just get to a place where it’s harmful or can cause these harms because it exists? For somebody doing a cyberattack with the model, it’s not about choosing a model. It doesn’t matter what model you choose. If Anthropic has safeguards in place, maybe it can’t. This is their approach currently. I’m just pushing on that. Yes, certainly the models where they make sure the safeguards are not removed and they’re putting a lot of effort in seeing how those models are being used, monitoring the activity, the near-term risk from those models is not gigantic. It’s more where somebody can remove all the safeguards where the cyberattacks are available today. When you say cyberattacks, is it the things like the Hugging Face breach and the other cybersecurity incidents we’ve seen out of the labs that have made you feel that thresholds have been crossed, and you need to speak up more about the controls that are needed? It’s the basic power of the models towards everything: social relationships, the job market, cyberattacks, bioterrorism attacks. We’ve crossed the threshold of capability that this is by far the most dangerous technology ever invented, and it’ll have pervasive effects in the economy and how war is fought. The industry is actually speaking up about that quite a bit, but the awareness or even the willingness to believe these things about, “This time is different,” once you get outside the industry, it’s very, very low. The OpenAI structure, which is, “We may choose never to productize this and to ignore the capitalistic incentives,” only works if there’s one company. Yes, we definitely should work on the control problem, but that’s not the imminent threat. There are three ways to solve the control problem and it overlaps somewhat with bad actors using today’s models and safeguards. One is to have an external set of things that get to look at all of the model’s actions and subject them to a symbolic, clearly stated constraint, like, “Did I expect you to send mail to strangers? Did I expect you to go take over a different computer cluster?” You can surround the model with something that has the resource and security logic, or you can write these models in a different way. Fundamentally, anything you leave out of the optimization function of [reinforcement learning] can be abused where you have an implicit notion, “Of course I didn’t mean for you to do it this way.” You can change the template of the model, or you can have a white box understanding of what’s inside the model and see where you’re getting those mistakes, and then it’s not as foreign. Today, we grow these models and we just see how they behave.
