How do you think that’s going to go? Is that in the same 20-year time period as the robots building our houses? Is it faster, or is it slower? This is my full-time job as chairman of the Gates Foundation. In fact, this month we do two weeks of strategy reviews to decide how we’ll spend our money next year on tuberculosis, HIV, and malaria. It’s fantastic how we’re using AI, not only on the drug discovery side, but also as we get the African languages to be properly understood by these models, which is an initiative we announced with 60 partners last month. Healthcare discovery and delivery will be incredibly accelerated by AI, and that’s very exciting — better seeds, better livestock genetics, weather forecast for farmers in Africa, who never had any of those things. So yes, that’s good stuff that AI enables during the remaining 19 years of the Gates Foundation. The good stuff does take time. Some of the things, like cyberattacks and the job market thing, even in the next two to three years, you’ll see significant negative effects in those two areas Do you think that that is enough to overcome people’s skepticism or fear of the AI models they encounter today? Because that is very much how the companies think about it. No, come on. I think it’s unfair to say they were totally on a doomer message and now they’ve switched to some other message. These two things are deeply entangled. AI will eventually be able to model human biology in such a way that you’ll understand the side effects of a drug without having to do human trials. We’re not there, but in the next five to 10 years, there’s a very good chance these in silico models will give us that capability. Biology is very complex, AI is extremely good given enough data, and there are new tools to gather and feed it more data. It’s very, very good at dealing with complex systems, so it’s almost perfect for biology. But no, that doesn’t mean that people will feel good about the downsides. How quickly do the good things arrive? How quickly do you look at your medical bill and go, “Wow, that’s really low”? Or you say, “I want to buy a new house, wow, these houses are sure cheap”? The good stuff does take time. Some of the things, like cyberattacks and the job market thing, even in the next two to three years, you’ll see significant negative effects in those two areas. You have a number of ideas for how you would stop those negative effects, or prevent or at least mitigate them, in your letter. And they’re not small. You have a quote here that says, “We need a response far greater than the United States national security response in the wake of 9/11.” You want to set up an international body that somehow makes everyone do the right thing. These are challenging ideas. Absolutely. When we blew off nuclear bombs in World War II, did we expect that we’d go 80 more years without that happening? I’d give us a good grade on that. Things like caring for poor countries and helping them out — although right now we’re not prioritizing that as much as I think we should or we did in the past, we’ve done a very good job on that. This will be a greater challenge than anything humanity’s ever achieved. One of the things that strikes me about the comparison to nuclear, which has come up quite a bit on Decoder recently, is the government built the nuclear weapons. Sure, they had some contractors, but these were government projects and eventually the technology was in control of the government, and we never let regular people have it. Think about Muse in the context of nuclear weapons. Do you think it’s a mistake to have consumer agentic AI products like Muse and Dots that might not have the controls? Muse happens to be powered by Meta’s model, which is still a closed-source model. I do think allowing people through open weights to remove all safeguards is irresponsible in terms of the cyber and bio risk that comes from that. For these agents, there’ll be some early problems in terms of how they’re set up, but I fundamentally think people will have personal agents. Muse is a very nice step forward. I talked to Alexandr Wang about it, congratulated him, and even though it’ll have challenges, personal agents will help people get things done. Muse is the first truly great consumer AI product. One of the reasons people don’t like AI a lot in a diffuse way is they experience cheap free models and AI overviews or they use the free ChatGPT and it hallucinates and it lies to them, but Muse actually does things and it’s a good consumer product. But I wonder — in the nuclear age, we were not saying, “Everyone is going to have a nuclear reactor at home that we haven’t built the safety guards for.” There’s something here. The notion of letting everybody make smallpox at home or make nuclear weapons at home, of course, and you can parody some of these people saying, “Don’t worry about safeguards.” It’s taking that position. “We are not serious about the safety things.” But the personal agent thing is an expected development. AIs are really cool just as I intellectually explore things, even the work of the Gates Foundation. I’m far more empowered. But I agree with you, it’ll get out to more people in this personal agent form. The notion that liability laws are how we’re going to manage AI safety will be looked back on as absurd All the models we’ve been talking about right now effectively run on data centers owned by big companies. One of the arguments here is, “If they do something really wrong, you can just sue them and that will provide a deterrent or at least an incentive to create safety systems.” I’m not sure product liability is the way to go, but it has come up a lot that product liability itself will keep the industry in line. Then there’s the flip side, which is that you can just go buy a Mac Studio and run an open source and open weight model on it and it can do whatever you want. I’m not sure where any of that liability would lie or what would control or align the behaviors of those open source models. Do you think that that is an ever-growing risk? Do you think that that can be controlled? The notion that liability laws are how we’re going to manage AI safety will be looked back on as absurd. When bad people do bioattacks, you don’t go to the court and say, “Oh, okay.” Even for finding who is doing these attacks, the liability model fails in every respect because it’s not the AI companies themselves. It’s the people who take particularly the models without safeguards and do these things. We will take AI safety more seriously than we take airline safety, drug safety, and car safety. That is guaranteed. Whether it’s going to be enough... This is harder, harder than nuclear weapons, harder than any of those other domains. But what are you going to do about an open source model running on a PC that somebody built with just a random Nvidia graphics card in it? I would hope that with models above a certain capability, we find a way to require the safeguards and monitoring to be maintained in those models. I admit as time goes on that, if we don’t fairly soon decide to do that, you’re going to have a level of capability that’s out in dark corners that you won’t be able to see. A sovereign country says that models that are capable of sophisticated cyberattacks have to have safeguards kept in them. You can be open in the sense that you’re free, you can be open in the sense that you’re totally customizable, but you have to stay on platforms where, as you customize it, it’s checking to see if you’re customizing out the safeguards and monitoring and refusing to let you do that. So it means sovereigns go and say, “Are these trusted platforms able to perform this function or not?” I’m asking very nuts and bolts. Where would you technically implement that regulation? Sure, but you have to put them somewhere. Is it at the Hugging Face, which distributes some of these models? Is it at the chip level? No, it’s exactly what I said: Models above a certain capacity can only be run on platforms that certify that, as they’re customized, it’s not taking out the safeguards. I admit as time goes on that, if we don’t fairly soon decide to do that, you’re going to have a level of capability that’s out in dark corners that you won’t be able to see Just to pull this apart, maybe I’m not quite getting it — is the Mac Studio a platform in that case that would have to pay attention to what code it’s running? You would not let a model past a certain capability be copied down to a client machine unless you’d somehow figured out how to monitor down to that client machine, which is very doable. But in the near term, most of these really frontier models are going to run more on Hugging Face or you could have them on GitHub. You can have all sorts of people be in the business of providing the platforms that maintain safeguards. Just to get this right, it seems like you would enforce the regulation at the platform and distribution level and not so much at the chip level. Because it feels to me at some point even the cheap, free, open source models will be capable enough as the models we have today. If those are open weights in such a way that all safeguards can be removed, then you’re going to have a very high level of cyberattacks and bioattacks. You can then say, “Okay, let’s rewrite all the code that we have today to be less subject to attack or let’s do biosurveillance in a very deep way,” which are things I believe we need to do in addition to these other restrictions. We’re proposing again a lot of new capabilities, right? We’re going to impose some restrictions on GitHub. GitHub does not have a lot of restrictions on what code it can distribute today. It has some, but it doesn’t have this kind of set of restrictions. When you put a state-of-the-art model that’s capable of profound cyberattacks or bioattacks on GitHub, it would say, “I’ve gone to the U.S. government and certified that I don’t let people customize out the safeguards.” I do not think that we currently live in a time of high trust in institutions. It just doesn’t feel that way to me. In fact, mostly we’ve torn them down. Elon Musk showed up and he tore down USAID. I know you have strong feelings about that. We have mostly torn down our institutions, for good and bad. No, that’s a little too strong. Our institutions- Somewhat weakened. It’s a very nice way of putting it. Trust in them is very low. We could probably do another full hour on AI drug discovery and whether a significant part of the population will trust the products of that work because they don’t trust pharma companies today. They don’t have to trust pharma companies today. They have to trust the FDA review process. I’m not sure they trust that either. Across the board, trust is low. Then they can walk away from taking them. When they get cancer, they can say, “No, I don’t want to take that cancer drug.” As far as I know, people do take drugs. It’s a thriving business and people take them because they have proven benefits. All I’m saying is, right now it feels like trust in institutions, particularly in America but around the world, is in decline. You are proposing a new class of institutions, potentially a new class of global institutions that might impose restrictions that are novel. At some point we need to say open source models, open weight models cannot do things and the government is going to reach into them and say whether or not they can do the things. No, they’ll make sure that the platforms don’t allow the safeguards to be taken away. How do you regain the institutional trust to impose these controls? Let’s say we don’t do this and we let models go off and do cyberattacks and bioattacks. Do you think that’s going to increase trust or human wellbeing? That’s the alternative. This is why I’m asking about it. This is why I asked about my kids and nihilism. I truly wonder about this. I’m not sure we’ll succeed at this, but I guarantee you this is doable. The FDA is very complex. The airline safety regulations are very complex. Now with AI, you can do an even better job with the same number of people in those organizations. It’s this weirdly recursive thing where AI will be involved in AI safety and auditing and a variety of things like that. Yes, this requires taking action, and I’m suggesting it’s only a matter of how bad the incidents are before we take action. People shouldn’t take everything I do at face value. I have all sorts of mistakes that I’ve made, weaknesses that I have, biases I bring to these questions. They ought to just take what I’m saying and add it to the voices I can sense your urgency about the harms. I can also sense your enthusiasm about the benefits over the long term, but we’re sitting here talking about trust. We’re going to put this on YouTube. I have all sorts of mistakes that I’ve made, weaknesses that I have, biases I bring to these questions. They ought to just take what I’m saying and add it to the voices. I’ve been in the technology industry thinking about AI my whole life, writing software, thinking about the power of software. I admit I was very fascinated as AI started to finally make progress. It was actually pretty slow until recently. I’m amazed and it’s actually helping me do my job in some incredible ways. But yes, I’m just one voice, an imperfect voice. I doubt you’ll find a perfect voice, but people can judge it however they like. I would say voices people trust are in short supply. When you go and talk to the people in this industry, it’s a bunch of young CEOs building huge companies. That is something you have some experience with. Do you think they’re going about this the right way? Do you think that they have the right intentions? Our problem today is not in the industry. Our problem is not that Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, Satya Nadella, or Sundar Pichai don’t understand that there are some grave challenges here. I talk less to Elon than other people, but even his public statements about summoning the demon are exactly what we’re discussing here. Why is there not the political energy to do the things you’re suggesting? As you said, the government didn’t fund this work. This is private sector company work. The government is not the key customer for this work, not like rockets and weapons and things like that. When somebody comes along and says all other previous technology advances are trivial compared to this one, people should have a high degree of skepticism about that. Is life really going to be significantly different 10 years from now because of AI? People should say, “Those guys just got overexcited about this one thing.” But I’m here saying, with whatever credibility I have, that absolutely this is the most profound — both in terms of good and in terms of bad — technology ever invented. I’m not financially motivated on this. I’m suggesting some precautions and safeguards are absolutely needed. What do you make of the difference in the tenor of conversation in the United States versus in China, where it’s often reported that optimism around AI is much higher? Why do you think there’s a difference there? Most of those people don’t spend that much time in China. The concern about job markets in China is very, very high and you can’t characterize China as one monolith. There are the private companies doing the AI work like we have in the United States and then there’s the Chinese government. For example, when it came to whether crypto should be allowed, they said no. Should young people be gambling all the time? They said no. When it came to whether kids should have relationships with AIs, they said no. On a lot of these things, including usage of AI, they’re showing a willingness to do things on behalf of society. I’m here saying, with whatever credibility I have, that absolutely this is the most profound — both in terms of good and in terms of bad — technology ever invented. I’m not financially motivated on this The fervor is a tiny bit less there, because their economy isn’t as AI-centric as ours is right at the moment. In the end, [when it comes to] the human value of not wanting cyberattacks or bioterrorism or trying to figure out what do we do when jobs go away, I don’t think there’s some difference between Chinese people and Americans and all the rest of the humans in terms of how they’re going to view this. Do you think it’s going to take something actually bad happening to get the political capital of the United States to make these changes? Probably yes. We get ready for earthquakes because there are small earthquakes. I thought after we had the COVID pandemic, that would cause us to be more ready for future pandemics. That one didn’t work the way I expected. But usually that galvanizes our attention. Over the next several years, certainly in the cyber domain, because there’s a business model there, you will see a lot of attacks different in terms of scale than anything we’ve seen in the past. There is one thing that, again, I’ll come back to and I just want to end here. I am worried about young people and nihilism. I sense it in our audience all the time. We have a young audience. There are some things about AI that are just legitimately the coolest things I’ve ever seen a computer do. There’s something there that can be fun and interesting and fascinating and change your entire relationship with the very notion of living in a digital world. Then there are obviously horrible things. What I see more often than not is a sense of distrust, not just in institutions, but also in the companies, in capitalism itself, in the notion that we should change how we tax labor and capital, which I know is another one of your ideas. The idea that, I don’t know, we should even have more kids. It’s all done. They’re going to kill us all and we should just take a beat. There is a lot of nihilism out there. I feel it very keenly. If you were a young person today and you were listening to Bill Gates say it’s about 20 years of disruption and at the end of it, robots might be building houses, what would you hold onto as a source of hope? Throughout human history, we’ve been dealing with the shortage of food and disease and war. [Historian] Yuval Noah Harari writes eloquently about the past. And it is ironic that now that we have this sort of Pandora’s box that will take food shortage — which is still a real thing, not in the U.S., but in Africa — will take diseases, where human suffering is unbelievable, and solve all these things, it comes with it such an effect on jobs and our sense of unique contribution. I think, “The way I orchestrate resources to eradicate malaria, I’m pretty unique in my life experience that lets me do that.” But when the AI says to me, “No, I’m better than you. You go play pickleball and I’ll get back to you. I’m going to take care of this,” it’s humorous, but it’s not humorous. For somebody who is young today and wants to make a unique contribution, it is very intimidating that the AI is so powerful. That’s why the crisis in the math community is going to be mirrored everywhere. If you’re in your twenties today, during this 20-year period, you can take the things AI still can’t do, and you can use AI and you can have a lot of good impact, and you can suggest policies. We may need to develop the taste to actually not use technology to its maximum. Even in the very best case of how we manage AI, this is going to be as tumultuous a period as there’s been in human history When I was talking with Claude about human-reserved job categories and trying to get that up to, say, 40 percent of the labor market — so you could use early retirement, restricted work weeks, and things like that to still get something like we have today — its response was always, “You’re artificially restricting the AI from doing these things.” I couldn’t get to a very high percentage without doing that. We’re going to have real choices. We have had groups — not large groups, but like the Amish — who don’t live at the technology frontier. A lot of humanity may choose not to live at the technological frontier because of things we get from helping each other or coming up with unique ideas. On the idea that young people are concerned because of the disruption that’s coming — that even in the very best case of how we manage AI, this is going to be as tumultuous a period as there’s been in human history — I hate being the bearer of concerning news on this, but it’s even worse not to tell people that this is coming. We have choices as humans to make about how we shape it. Bill, I appreciate you sounding the alarm and I appreciate you being on Decoder. Thank you so much. Questions or comments? Apple will label apps that are ‘Made for iPhone Duo’

Apple will label apps that are ‘Made for iPhone Duo’

The Verge | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Many apps, including Netflix, Microsoft Word, and Zoom, are taking advantage of the Duo’s extra screen real estate. Many apps, including Netflix, Microsoft Word, and Zoom, are taking advantage of the Duo’s extra screen real estate. Apple is rolling out a new badge in the App Store to indicate whether an app is optimized for its new foldable. The new “Made for iPhone Duo” badge will appear across apps and games that work with the device’s extra screen real estate. Though Apple doesn’t include an image of what this badge might look like, it highlighted some of the ways apps are taking advantage of the Duo’s inner and outer screens. Netflix, for example, will display shows and movies that take up the whole inner display, while allowing you to watch its feed of short-form clips on the outside. Amazon’s Prime Video will let you watch shows and movies with the Duo partially folded, with the content on top and details about the cast on the bottom. Meanwhile, Apple says Microsoft Word and Outlook will offer a “desktop-class” experience on the Duo, with Word placing its formatting tools alongside a document, and Outlook displaying your inbox next to a full email. Zoom can also display a video conference alongside shared content across the iPhone Duo’s inner screen. Other apps optimized for the Duo include Paramount Plus, NYT Cooking, Yelp, Lyft, and United Airlines. But, as The Verge’s Nilay Patel found during his Duo testing, some apps “optimized” for the device, like Instagram, still have some bugs. Apple says it’s going to launch editorial collections for the iPhone Duo on Friday, “with additional app updates rolling out throughout the year.” The iPhone Duo starts at $1,999 and launches on Friday, with “limited” quantities available on a walk-in basis. The Atari VCS saga will come to an end next year when its software store closes
2026-10-22

The Atari VCS saga will come to an end next year when its software store closes

The Verge | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Ten years after the console was announced, the Atari VCS platform will be shutting down in early 2027.﻿ Ten years after the console was announced, the Atari VCS platform will be shutting down in early 2027.﻿ Following a decade long saga of delays, name changes, and Atari having to assure backers of a crowdfunding campaign that its retro console wasn’t doomed, the Atari VCS platform is being retired early next year. On February 1st, 2027, the Atari VCS software store is shutting down, according to an announcement posted to the Atari Club Discord and shared by Tom’s Hardware. With a design loosely inspired by the original Atari 2600 — minus a cartridge slot — the VCS was a Linux-based, AMD-powered mini PC that could emulate a large collection of pre-loaded classic Atari games, but its functionality could be expanded through an online software store that included access to streaming platforms, and in recent years, game streaming platforms like Xbox Cloud Gaming and Nvidia GeForce Now. Atari VCS owners will still be able to download and add software to their libraries through Atari’s official online store up until January 31st, 2027. After that the store will officially shut down, but previously downloaded apps and games will still be usable. Atari says that following the store’s closure it plans to “make an OS update available that allows you to migrate all functionality to your local machine, removing any dependencies on Atari infrastructure for authentication and software.” The company also plans to release an Atari Home Brew app allowing owners to continue to add new games to their console’s library shared to the community by third-party developers who still want to develop and support the VCS. Insta360 launches a cheaper version of its wireless mics with color screens
2026-10-22

Insta360 launches a cheaper version of its wireless mics with color screens

The Verge | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
The Insta360 Mic is a more affordable alternative to the Mic Pro and doesn’t sacrifice many features. The Insta360 Mic is a more affordable alternative to the Mic Pro and doesn’t sacrifice many features. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Following the launch of its wireless Mic Pro system last May featuring built-in color E Ink screens for custom branding and an array of three microphones to emulate different pickup patterns, Insta360 has announced a cheaper alternative that skips a few features many content creators might not miss. The new Insta360 Mic sits between the company’s Mic Pro and the budget-friendly Mic Air, which uses much smaller screenless transmitters. While the Mic Pro is priced at $329.99 for a kit that includes two microphone transmitters, one wireless receiver, and a charging case, the Insta360 Mic costs $249.99 for a similar bundle. Insta360 sells the microphones individually since they can directly connect to smartphones and many of the company’s cameras (including the X6, X5, and Luna Ultra) over Bluetooth. The Mic Pro transmitter on its own costs $99.99, while the new Insta360 Mic is $79.99. The design and functionality of the Insta360 Mic is very similar to the Mic Pro. It has the same 1.22-inch, six-color E Ink display on the front that can be customized with logos, photos, or solid colors to help it blend into clothing. The transmitters have a range of up to 400 meters when paired with Insta360’s wireless receiver that can connect to professional cameras, laptops, or other recording devices using a built-in USB-C connector. The Insta360 Mic also features AI-powered noise canceling to reduce background noise and automatic gain control modes that adjust recording levels on the fly to help prevent clipping if things suddenly get too loud. So what are you missing out on when choosing the cheaper Insta360 Mic? The biggest downgrade is that the transmitters have just a single mic instead of the triple mic array in the Mic Pro, so you cannot select from multiple pickup patterns; omnidirectional is the only option. However, that simplified hardware makes the transmitters slightly lighter than the Mic Pro’s — 19.4 grams compared with 19.7 grams — and battery life increases from up to 10 hours to up to 13 hours, which can be boosted to 34 hours with the charging case. The Mic Pro’s transmitters come with 32GB of built-in storage for creating a backup recording. The Insta360 Mic transmitters don’t have any built-in storage, but you can enable backup recording by sticking a microSD card inside each one. As with the lighter design and improved battery life, the microSD option also feels like an upgrade for the Insta360 Mic if you want more than 32GB of storage. The Birdbuddy 2 Mini is now called the Birdbuddy Nook, and it is designed to be a smaller and more affordable option the company describes as its “most accessible smart bird feeder yet.” The flagship Birdbuddy 2 has been renamed the Birdbuddy Home and delivers upgrades like a redesigned camera with a wider field of view and integrated solar panels. Both models are now available through Birdbuddy’s online store while some configurations will be available at other retailers later this month. The Birdbuddy Nook starts at $99 and features a redesigned circular 2K HDR camera with a 135-degree field of view. The camera wakes up the moment a bird lands to help ensure visits are captured. The camera can shoot in landscape or portrait orientations, making it easier to share videos and images on social media, while the AI-powered Birdbuddy app identifies exactly what species is visiting. The Nook’s 3.8-cup seed reservoir may need frequent refills if a lot of birds regularly stop by, but you can avoid frequent battery charges by upgrading to the $159 Nook Solar, which adds a solar panel to the roof. For an extra $40 on top of that, the $199 Nook Extended Edition includes a larger perch to accommodate more birds. The $219 Birdbuddy Home uses the same 2K HDR camera with a 135-degree field of view as the Nook, but in a larger feeder designed to require less maintenance. It has a six-cup seed reservoir and comes with a pair of solar panels on top to help keep the battery charged, assuming the feeder’s installed somewhere with adequate sun exposure. The Home also has a built-in perch extender to accommodate more visitors, dual-band Wi-Fi, and an upgraded mic that can help the mobile app more accurately identify a species through its birdsong. Googlebooks just aren’t ready
2026-10-22

Googlebooks just aren’t ready

The Verge | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Our collective reviews of every Googlebook at launch: very good laptops hobbled by beta-level software. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. EEvery Googlebook laptop we’ve tested so far has great hardware. The problem is the software. While Googlebook OS has exciting ideas for how a laptop and phone can work together, right now it’s mostly a frustrating experience filled with annoying bugs and lackluster app support. It often feels like using an Android tablet with a keyboard and trackpad grafted on, rather than a cohesive laptop OS. It’s too bad, because those keyboards and trackpads — and pretty much all the laptops’ hardware components — are great. Frankly, they should be. The cheapest is $900, a price where you start to expect quality processors, metal builds, higher-quality displays, good speakers, and great keyboards and trackpads (even haptic ones in some cases). The Googlebooks have those covered, and they also have very good battery life and at least 16GB of RAM. Google wanted Googlebooks to feel premium, and HP, Acer, Lenovo, Dell, and Asus all delivered. It’s Google’s software that needs to catch up. In their current state, we don’t recommend you buy any of them. But it’s worth reviewing the hardware to determine which shows the most promise if the software one day improves — or if crafty modders manage to install other operating systems. Since the software is the same on all Googlebooks (save for some preinstalled manufacturer apps), and we’ve already covered that software at length, we’re strictly talking about Googlebook hardware here. The scores, however, cover hardware and software as they stand today. Brace yourself — the scores are bad. If you’re a dedicated Google user or Pixel fan and you really want to be an early adopter / paying beta tester — or if you’re just here for some schadenfreude — here are our collective thoughts on each and every Googlebook. The most affordable of the Googlebooks (for now)Nice screen and build Googlebook OS feels like a betaNo portrait orientation in tablet mode The Acer Googlebook 14 is the only 2-in-1 convertible of the bunch, and it’s one of the nicest-feeling and -looking Acers I’ve ever used at this price. It’s light, with a sturdy metal chassis and a great 2880 x 1800 / 120Hz OLED touchscreen with stylus support. I find 2-in-1s pretty handy, even if they look clunkier than tablets like a Microsoft Surface Pro or an iPad with a keyboard case, but Googlebook OS doesn’t yet support portrait orientation, which limits the Acer’s stylus potential. Like all Googlebooks, the Acer has an Auto-Rotate toggle in settings, but it only flips the orientation 180 degrees — a strange oversight. The speakers don’t have a lot of bass, and they can get muffled in tablet mode depending on how you hold the laptop, but they’re decent and sound balanced overall. The webcam is a slight weak point, as colors and contrast are a touch drab. But nothing here is bad. This isn’t like some of the Acer Aspires or Swifts I’ve tested in the past where you had to deal with a terrible screen and shoddy build quality for the sake of a good chip. It’s just an all-around nice package. That extends to the battery life, which lasted me eight to nine hours of light-to-mixed usage. If this was running Windows or a proper Linux distro with the same hardware and price, it’d be a rare bright spot in our ongoing RAMageddon hellscape. But thanks to Googlebook OS, it’s not. And an Acer rep told us its $900 launch price is only available for a limited time. That might sound like an incentive to buy one before a price increase, but please allow me to tap the “Don’t buy it now for what it might do later” sign. Actually, picture me tapping it as you read this whole article. — Antonio G. Di Benedetto, Laptop Reviewer Small and lightOn the cheaper side of GooglebooksGreat keyboard and trackpad Googlebook OS feels like a betaAnti-glare display has some issuesOnly the two USB-C ports Here’s my theory: If Googlebooks are going to work anytime soon, they’re going to work as secondary computers. Maybe they don’t need to replace your primary work machine if they can instead be light, fast, simple, and fun to use. (Yes, I agree that $900 or more is a lot to pay for this kind of device, but hey, iPads pull it off!) I already see glimmers of possibility here, even through the bugs of Googlebook OS. “A Chromebook, but also it runs all my apps” is not a bad pitch for an off-day computer. That’s why, of all the available Googlebooks, the Dell XPS Googlebook makes the most sense to me. At 2.2 pounds, with a 13.4-inch screen, and starting at $999, it’s one of the smallest and lightest Googlebooks available. (And unlike the cheaper Acer Googlebook 14, it doesn’t offer a bunch of flippy tablet modes that don’t really work.) Like the other models we’ve tested, it’s a lovely piece of high-end hardware; I love the keyboard and trackpad, I appreciate that I can open the thing with one hand, and the Qualcomm chip inside does its job fine for all my practical purposes. The battery got me through a full day of travel and then some. My only real complaint is with the anti-glare coating on the display, which does help tamp down reflections but also creates that slight rainbow-shimmer effect that makes the screen appear a little less sharp and clear. The coating also dims the screen a bit — I’ve used the XPS Googlebook mostly at max brightness, just to get it up to normal. I am generally extremely in favor of these matte displays, but I’d trade the XPS Googlebook’s for a glossy one in a heartbeat. As we keep saying, there’s simply no way around the fact that Googlebook OS isn’t ready. Google is fixing and improving it quickly (adding the ability to run any website as an app was a big win), but I can’t get past the apps that don’t work properly with a keyboard, don’t work on this size screen at all, or simply don’t exist on Googlebook OS (bring back Linux apps, now!). If you’re in the market for an overpriced second computer and you want easy access to Google Messages and your Drive files, a Googlebook might be exactly what you need. Someday. Maybe by then Dell will have fixed the screen. Googlebook OS feels like a betaOne of the heaviest GooglebooksLimited ports HP’s Googlebook 14 is more proof that of all the problems facing these early Googlebook models, the hardware isn’t one of them. Starting from $1,199.99, this Googlebook is about the same size and weight as a MacBook Air 13, despite fitting in a larger 14-inch display. It’s a great panel, too: a 2880 x 1800 resolution, 120Hz, OLED touchscreen with a slim bezel, good viewing angles, and punchy brightness. The Snapdragon X Elite chip has proved reliable so far (and for what it’s worth, my Asus model with an Intel chip has been solid too, despite early concerns), with 16GB of RAM and the option to bump memory up to 32GB and storage as high as 1TB, but I haven’t pushed it through anything more demanding than image editing in the infuriating mobile versions of Lightroom and Photoshop. The body is mostly metal, so the whole thing feels tough and solid. The keyboard’s comfortable and the haptic trackpad expansive, and the privacy shutter on the webcam is always a welcome touch. There are only two USB-C ports and a headphone jack, so grab one of the other Googlebooks if you know you need HDMI or USB-A. I’m a fan of the Googlebook-specific hardware touches too, which you’ll find across the lineup. I’m already building the habit of using the Caps Lock key for its Quick Insert function instead, with easy access to features like the clipboard, emoji, and Rambler dictation — though the latter gets its own dedicated function key too. It’s joined by keys for the classic Android navigation buttons and a shortcut for some flexible screenshot and screen recording options. The Glowbar is a little less impressive; it’s a cute design but doesn’t actually do very much, and since it’s on the lid you’ll rarely see it in action anyway. From the hardware side this feels like a $1,200 laptop through and through — it’s the software where we still have questions. Lots of portsGreat keyboard and a matte OLED display Googlebook OS feels like a betaMost expensive Googlebook (currently) Asus’ entry in the first slate of Googlebooks was able to keep up with me better than I expected, with solid hardware that helped soften the blow of software frustrations. At $1,299, the Asus Googlebook 14 is the priciest of the first five Googlebooks. The base configuration has an Intel Core Ultra 5 325 CPU, 16GB of RAM, and 256GB of storage (you can also get the 512GB model for the same price at Best Buy). I rarely felt like I was pushing those specs to their limit — the Asus got pretty toasty when I was running games on it, but that’s true of just about any laptop. It breezed through things like managing a dozen or more Chrome tabs and hopping between a few different apps at once. Asus says the 70Wh battery lasts up to 18 hours, and while I didn’t get quite that long, I didn’t have trouble getting through a typical day without charging. One major plus is the Asus’ wealth of ports: two USB-C, two USB-A, a headphone jack, and even HDMI. That’s more than most of the other Googlebooks have, or my MacBook for that matter. I was able to hook up the Asus to my monitor, keyboard, mouse, and a wired controller without needing a USB-C hub or a docking station. I mainly used the Asus without my external monitor, though, largely because it has a fantastic keyboard — it’s satisfyingly clicky with good key travel and tactile feedback. The 14-inch OLED (2880 x 1800) display doesn’t disappoint, either. It has a 120Hz refresh rate and a nice matte finish. The few games that ran well on the Asus, like the Android version of Dredge, looked great. The Asus Googlebook 14 has some real highs, but the current Googlebook OS experience makes it a tough sell at $1,299. For the same price, you could get the 13-inch M5 MacBook Air with similar specs or, for $100 more, you could also get Asus’ new Windows ExpertBook A7. Sizable OLED in a lightweight laptopSleek color and finish Googlebook OS feels like a betaKeys are a little wobbly, making for an only okay typing feelAI Mouse is basically just an RGB mouse The $1,099 Lenovo Googlebook 15 is the largest of the launch lineup, and it’s low-key my favorite. I love a bigger screen, especially in a lightweight laptop. At around 2.8 pounds / 1.27kg, the Lenovo Googlebook is nearly half a pound lighter than Apple’s 15-inch MacBook Air. It’s not as thin or nice as a MacBook Air, but it makes up for that with more ports (HDMI 2.1, two USB-C, and one USB-A). It also has a bright 2880 x 1800 / 120Hz OLED display and a large haptic trackpad that feels and sounds like a tight drum when you click it. Despite that sizable OLED, the battery easily lasted me an eight-hour workday of navigating Chrome tabs with a handful of extra apps like Apple Music and Signal running. And its light gray color and satiny finish is just charming. Of the three Googlebooks I’ve tested (I tried the HP as well), this has been the one I used the most because of that larger screen and satisfying trackpad. The speakers are good too, with a touch of stereo separation due to the laptop’s size, and they get surprisingly loud. On the other hand, the keyboard is only fine. It’s got a nice clacky feel, but the keycaps are slightly wobbly, and overall it lacks the tight, satisfying tactility of other Lenovo laptop keyboards (like ThinkPads). And while I love this laptop’s off-white color, the bright white keyboard backlight sometimes makes the legends harder to see unless you’re actually in a dark space (this is one place RGB backlighting would actually help). Also included with the Lenovo Googlebook 15 (at least, if you buy it directly from Lenovo or Google) is an AI Mouse. Uhh. It’s a Bluetooth mouse. It’s got a light-up button for launching Gemini. That’s about all that makes it an “AI Mouse.” Having a Glowbar that actually faces you seems like a nice idea, but just like the ones built into the lid of every Googlebook, the one on the mouse doesn’t do much. You can pick different lighting colors and patterns in the preinstalled Lenovo Vantage app, and the Glowbar does a brief colorful flourish when you summon Gemini — but that’s about it. It’s just a fine ergonomic mouse that matches this fine laptop hardware. And that’s the story with every Googlebook so far: The hardware is fine, and even great. But until the software is worthy of the hardware, that’s just not good enough. — Antonio G. Di Benedetto, Laptop Reviewer A free daily digest of the news that matters most. This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. On a December evening in 1959, the physicist Richard Feynman gave a lecture called “There’s Plenty of Room at the Bottom.” There is no physical law stopping us from writing the entire Encyclopædia Britannica on the head of a pin, he argued. We simply had not built the tools yet. He was right, and for decades, computing took us into that space almost for free, as transistors repeatedly halved in size. But that easy descent has now stalled. Today, the industry is increasingly turning to brute force, building ever-bigger data centers to boost performance. Yet the laws of physics leave plenty of room for improvement. Here’s how researchers are exploring ways to unlock the next leap in computing. Maybe, like me, you’ve been aware that this layer of frozen soil is thawing, that it’s important in the global puzzle of climate change … and that’s about the extent of it. The great news for both of us is that my colleague James Temple just helicoptered into Alaska’s forests to report on the fires threatening permafrost and their complicated potential climate effects. Here are three things I took away from his reporting. This story is from The Spark, our weekly climate tech newsletter. Sign up to receive it in your inbox every Wednesday. MIT Technology Review
Making the case for context-aware AI
2026-10-22

Making the case for context-aware AI

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Enterprise AI can generate recommendations, automate tasks, and help organizations make decisions faster. But without an understanding of how a business actually operates, even powerful models can miss the constraints and relationships that determine whether a decision is the right one. As companies look to move AI from experimentation into everyday operations, the challenge ahead is giving technology the context to act usefully within complex business processes. For Devavrat Shah, chief scientist of enterprise AI at Celonis, that context is central to what he calls “decision intelligence.” AI agents can support individual tasks within larger decision-making processes, he argues, but they need to understand the role each task plays and the business conditions surrounding it. “You can't just do that in a vacuum,” he says. Without the right operational context, agents risk overlooking important constraints, making costly mistakes, or consuming excessive computing resources as they try to work out what they need to know. The solution begins with connecting AI to the processes and data enterprises already use. A contextual layer can help agents understand workflows, operate within defined guardrails, and produce recommendations that people can review and approve. Shah advises organizations to start with a specific business decision, identify its most difficult steps, and test whether AI can improve them. Measuring gains in speed and value can then help determine whether an application is worth scaling. This approach could also change how employees spend their time. Automating repetitive, manual work and coordinating tasks across teams could free people to focus on more complex strategic decisions. Realizing that benefit, however, will require investment in workforce education, so employees can understand AI outputs and their implications instead of just handing decisions over to agents. Looking to the future, Shah sees opportunities extending from routine operational tasks to enterprise-wide planning in areas such as finance, workforce management, marketing, and sales. With the right context, AI could help organizations evaluate options and make consequential decisions more quickly. As Shah puts it, “Now, maybe [resource planning] should not take that much time, and people are actually making the right choices quickly.” This episode of Business Lab is produced in partnership with Celonis. Megan Tatum: From MIT Technology Review, I'm Megan Tatum, and this is Business Lab, the show that helps business leaders make sense of new technologies coming out of the lab and into the marketplace.This podcast is produced in partnership with Celonis.Now, enterprise AI is at something of a crossroads. After years of experimentation, demos, and pilots, leadership teams want clear proof that scaling up will positively impact their bottom line, but many are missing an integral part of the puzzle. By acting without operational context, enterprise AI and AI agents are performing at a significant deficit. They're applying generalized principles to specialized scenarios and coming up short, leaving business leaders understandably unconvinced and AI's potential unrealized.Two words for you: context matters.My guest today is Devavrat Shah, chief scientist of enterprise AI at Celonis.Welcome, Devavrat. Devavrat Shah: Hello, Megan. Thanks for having me here. Megan: Thank you so much for being here with us today. Devavrat, just to start, why is it so crucial that organizations are thinking about operational context as it relates to enterprise AI right now? What makes it such a critical part of that transition I was talking about from experimentation to scale? Devavrat: It's a fantastic question. And as you correctly noted, we're at the cusp of becoming experimental to mainstream as far as AI is concerned. As you know, I've been teaching AI at MIT as a professor for more than the last two decades, so it's no surprise that I'm very bullish about AI's future in enterprises.The way I think about where AI can make the biggest difference in enterprises is in decision making, helping enterprise make decisions, or putting it other way, how do we bring decision intelligence to enterprises? As we think about decisions, how do we make decisions today? Take MIT Tech Review, take Celonis, take MIT, take any large organization. We have different types of decisions we want to make in very broad strokes. There are decisions which are strategic decisions like episodic, other decisions which are operational that need to be done with a high frequency. In either of them, we have some processes in place. You do this first, you do that first, you do that first, you do that first. Many of us help coordinate those things. Many of us play roles within it, what Jensen Huang calls task. And once you get those things done step by step, then you reach decision.Now, it's those tasks, or what I would call roles, that's where AI can bring in an atomic manner agentic power so that we can at least parts of decisions, if not all of the decisions, we can augment support and maybe sometimes when it's a painful automate. And to get to that, to know which role to do, which task to do, we really need to know the context. You can't just do that in a vacuum. And that's why context with AI put together in the right way would bring what I would call decision intelligence to enterprise. And that's where I think there'll be the biggest transformation that would come. Megan: Right. To drill down into that into a bit more detail, we know many organizations are, as you say, embracing identic AI right now with this potential for increasingly autonomous decision making. If context isn't introduced in parallel, what are the commercial risks at play? Devavrat: Fantastic. Let's just take the setting I was describing. We are trying to automate a role or support a role or augment a role depending on the type of decision, depending upon type of task. And let's for a second imagine that all these, as they're called, frontier labs have built these amazing models and amazing AI infrastructure so that everything that's happened in the universe and it's likely to happen in the universe is all there.Now, we have a small decision that we want to make for that very specific context is there. And without having access to that, this massive system, this extremely bright person would figure that out eventually by asking lots of questions, the way we do it as humans, and then finally do the right thing. The problem is to get to that, it will do so much compute at some level that to get simple tasks done, we are spending massive amount of compute. So that's scenario one. In reality, that's not going to be the scenario, and we'll miss some important context, important constraint, important guardrail, and then we'll make a mistake. And sometimes that mistake can be okay. It's like me giving you a recommendation saying, go restaurant A, and maybe you may not enjoy the dinner, versus sometimes mistakes will be very, very expensive. That is me telling you what the financial forecast is and somehow that leads to the company's doom. Megan: Right. So in an enterprise setting, these are really business critical decisions that are being made. So the impact is that much more if we get this wrong, isn't it? And on a practical level then, what are the different ways that organizations can add in this context layer we're talking about? How does Celonis, for example, deliver that operational context via a real time digital twin of an organization? Devavrat: Absolutely. I think if it might be useful just to double click on the history of Celonis. As you know, Celonis, as an organization, came into existence with the mission, with the goal of helping organizations adhere to their ideal processes, build better processes, enable people to work with multiple processes in an intertwined manner. And that's where their primary, let's call it a DNA, has been about what I would call process intelligence. Take how enterprises help put multiple things together, digitize them, enable people to get their processes better, understand where the issues are, and then how do you re-engineer the process within organizations so that things can happen better. Now, if you want to bring decision intelligence into this, each atomic step of this process that was potentially was done before by experts, people, now we want agents to help us enable that. And that's where knowing this context of a process within enterprise and then bringing AI agents along with that, what I would like to call it as a roled agents, exactly provide the right way to bring decision intelligence to enterprises. And this is how Celonis brings context to help enterprise make better decision. Megan: Right. And then in terms of how this works as well, how does the context layer relate to both the layer below, that's the systems of record layer, and also the layer above, that identic layer? Devavrat: That's a fantastic question, Megan. The layer below primarily is, if we call it system of record, primarily it's the workflow tools that are there and then the event logs that they generate around it. The layer above is either business user experts consuming information, or they're consuming that information maybe through agents working on top of that context layer. And this is where the way context model, as Celonis calls it, is designed is to do three things simultaneously. One is to get the right abstraction of the layer below, so that the right pieces of the process can be agentified. Two is to bring the rolled agents for each of these pieces in a structured guardrailed manner. And then the three is to be agent first so that if there are other agentic infrastructure that are already talking to it, they can naturally talk to it so that then they can bring the right output for humans or experts to review, look at it, approve, and maybe then at times automate. Megan: Fantastic. And for all the benefits that we're talking about with this, are there also challenges when it comes to implementing a context layer? For example, do most organizations have the right data in place to provide AI with the context it needs? And if not, I wonder what some of the practical steps are that they might need to take to address that. Devavrat: It's again, a crucial question to make this real. We can imagine an ideal world, but if we cannot reach the ideal world, then it's just ideal. So now in this setting, as we discussed the three layers, one being the layer which is the layer below system of record and workflow tools. In some sense, those things already exist. They don't need to be complete. They don't need to be in the cleanest possible manner. The way context model is designed with agentic's architecture, it manages to connect to those things and make sense of it. And then things on top is very much in control of Celonis's infrastructure, so it's not that complicated. The crucial thing here though is when one goes to enterprise, when one opens up, let's say a system of record or workflow tool, event logs or things like that, one has to go through, what I would call, classical InfoSec to make sure that you can trust a vendor's software that's coming into your organization. And I think that is the primary process that is a step between, before and after, if I may call it. Megan: Right. So that's the real crucial step for people to be taking here. And as AI agents with context are able to make more complex, more accurate hopefully decisions, I wonder what you think the role of human intelligence and input will be moving forward, and how can organizations perhaps strike the balance between the two? Devavrat: There are the benefits and then there are the challenges. Benefits are clear. If you have right AI agents in a right role manner going through your decision processes, hopefully they're doing it quicker and hence your decisions are taken earlier so that your opportunity cost is not lost. You are taking decisions in time, you can plan much better, et cetera, et cetera. This is especially useful when you have to take very manual, repetitive, grungy tasks where access to workforce, coordination between people become crucial parts of the decision process. In a sense, that's where the huge value can come in. The flip side of that is that means that people won't be doing that.On the other hand, the other type of decision that we always make, which are strategic decisions. It could be a small strategic decision, it could be large strategic decision, but the strategic decisions that typically most organizations don't have bandwidth to take to think, that opportunity would open up and that's where likely lots of humans and experts would engage more. In a sense, now they would have better support helping them rationally think through what are the choices that they have, how do different choices compare? And then at the end of the day, it's organization's role to decide which of the option to take because it belongs to their interest in mind where they want to go.And so in a sense, there will be what I would call shift of workforce in terms of instead of doing a lot more repetitive, a lot less repetitive, but a lot more strategic work and that's where people will have to spend time.The other place where I am personally very optimistic is like every technology change creates new work that it's difficult to predict at the time of change. My hope is that there will be another type of opportunity and work that will be created where we'll be engaging our workforce. Goes without saying, this would mean that we will have to carefully think through reeducating or educating some of our workforce thoughtfully. And I think as an academic in me, as an educator in me, I really feel that the timescale at which these changes are happening are so fast. So we need to also act in a similar manner. Megan: For any decision maker listening to this, how should they start implementing these changes? For example, how can they prepare their workforce? What role should governance play as well? Devavrat: Okay, that's a great question. If you want to start, the first thing you need to do is identify a decision where you want to bring intelligence. Let's identify the process around it. Let's identify each step of the process. Let's take the most crucial, difficult step in that. Let's try to bring AI agents to help you to do that. And then from there you go and then let's measure how much speed are we getting, how much value are we getting? And that'll help us understand whether this was worth it. So that's one. Second is governance. Well, we're going to bring AI in this prescriptive manner for each task in a role. That means that for that role, we can tell what it can or cannot do very clearly. And Celonis prescribes certified role agents for these things. This helps you clearly have an understanding of what sort of contract would be for this.And the third thing, which is people, and now this is where it's really important, is a part which gets automated, but then there's a part where experts will be in the loop and this is where they need to understand the outputs of AI, how do you work with that and what its implication would be? And this is where some AI education is needed and then some tool education is needed. Megan: Yes, absolutely. The pace is incredible, isn't it, in this space? And speaking of, I suppose if we could close with a look ahead, given the right context, of course, what are the use cases perhaps for enterprise AI that are on the horizon that you think could be most impactful or perhaps what you're simply most excited about? Devavrat: I think I would categorize them into three types. They're all decision intelligence, decision-making, but there are three types. One, what I would call many repetitive manual labor-intensive, so that's one.Two, what I would call coordination style tasks. A lot of work that we do right now as an information workforce that's spent in coordination, that's why email spreadsheets, et cetera. A lot of that would be done much better, much quicker and less boring, so to speak.And then the third is where, especially as a support to human decision-makers to provide strategic support augmentation, and that's a third type of decisions that would be done. If I think of any planning process in enterprises, so financial planning, workforce planning, marketing, sales, product, anything, all of those are business critical decisions that we make that take annual planning process, take annual time. Now maybe it should not take that much time, and people are actually making the right choices quickly. Megan: That'd be fantastic, wouldn't it? Fantastic. Thank you so much for that brilliant conversation, Devavrat. That was Devavrat Shah, chief scientist of enterprise AI at Celonis, whom I spoke with from Brighton in England.That's it for this episode of Business Lab. I'm your host, Megan Tatum. MIT Technology Review
Three things you should know about permafrost
2026-10-22

Three things you should know about permafrost

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Maybe, like me, you’ve been aware that this layer of frozen soil is thawing, that it’s important in the global puzzle of climate change … and that’s about the extent of it. The great news for both of us is that my colleague James Temple just published two in-depth stories about permafrost. His reporting took him to the boreal forests of Alaska, where he helicoptered into the wilderness to learn more about how this material could affect our climate both now and in the future. I’d recommend you give both of James’s stories a read. There’s this feature about efforts to tackle the intensifying fires that are threatening permafrost, as well as this look at how researchers are trying to understand the complicated tangle of potential climate effects. Here are three things I took away. Permafrost is the frozen layer of soil that’s under about 15% of land around the Northern Hemisphere. While technically material needs to be frozen for only two consecutive years to count as permafrost, most of our current permafrost formed during the last ice age. That means there’s organic material that’s been frozen, and essentially locked away, for thousands of years. But as global temperatures tick up and this layer thaws, microbes are able to break down that organic matter, releasing carbon dioxide and methane—powerful greenhouse gases that drive climate change. Those emissions form a feedback loop, accelerating warming further. The steady rise in global temperatures has already shrunk the footprint of the Arctic permafrost by about 12% since 1850, and the pace has been picking up in the last 50 years. The wild thing is, only a minority of the models that have been used in UN climate reports actually take into account the gradual loss of permafrost. And none of them include the abrupt thaw and collapse that can happen after events like heat waves and fires (more on those fires in a moment). There are efforts to make sure future UN reports reflect the growing science, but for now, all this means that we’re probably underestimating how much and how fast the planet is going to warm. Fire is a natural part of boreal forests’ life cycle, but they’re getting more intense because of climate change. And crucially, fires can accelerate the thawing of permafrost. By the end of the century, this effect could release the equivalent of 80 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide. Today, the policy across remote parts of Canada and Alaska is generally to let wildfires burn as long as they’re not threatening human habitats. But some researchers say we should explore fighting these fires to save the carbon trapped in the forests and the permafrost beneath them. Researchers at the Woodwell Climate Research Center are developing a map of burn risk and carbon vulnerability across Alaska, essentially highlighting areas where fires are most likely to spread and which areas are home to certain types of permafrost that would release the most emissions. (Yes, apparently there are different types of permafrost. Yedoma is a particularly deep, carbon-dense form from the late Pleistocene era.) That’s all just the beginning—once we figure out potential targets, the question is what to do about it. Some suggest setting more prescribed fires, something Indigenous groups have done for thousands of years, partly to reduce the danger of massive blazes. Or resources could go to early detection and efforts to put out fires before they grow. There’s about 1.5 trillion tons of carbon locked away in permafrost, about twice the amount in the atmosphere. We know that permafrost thawing can release greenhouse-gas emissions and drive climate change. But there are complications. One particularly big wrinkle: Other effects from permafrost thawing and fire recovery might counteract the carbon lost from those events. For example, as temperatures warm, plants can gradually move north and take hold where they couldn’t have before. Those plants soak up carbon from the atmosphere as they grow, at least partly locking away what was emitted. Researchers are working to better understand this ecosystem. And the more we learn, the more complicated the picture looks. How much carbon is lost from permafrost, and how much a given area will recover its carbon, depend on a huge array of factors. How severe are the fires that have accelerated local thawing? What’s the local climate and environment? And what kind of permafrost is at stake? The higher we push the planet’s temperature, the more we mess with complicated natural phenomena that we’re only beginning to understand. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
AI’s hidden storage bill: What inference leaves behind
2026-10-22

AI’s hidden storage bill: What inference leaves behind

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Ask most executives to picture the cost of AI and they’ll likely see GPUs—racks of accelerators with gigawatts of power—and a waitlist for silicon. But that picture leaves something out: Data storage costs. What happens to data after a model responds? Much of the back-end data that’s generated—from billions of daily prompts, images, and videos—needs to be stored. But data storage is one of the harder infrastructure costs to plan for, and it’s often overlooked in initial AI infrastructure planning. Traditionally, AI infrastructure conversations have centered on the compute-intensive process of training a model. Training is finite: a run starts, burns through compute, and ends. But inference runs continuously, every time a model answers a prompt. For infrastructure planning, that difference matters: A training run is a project you can budget for and retire. Inference doesn’t retire; every inference request leaves something behind. Take AI-generated video. In this workload, the back-end spend can be significant. From internal measurements, WD found that an 8-second clip (returned to a user as a 3.97MB 720p file) left more than 500MB of associated data behind on the back end, including logs, cached context, immediate renders, backups, and safety records. That’s a 130x increase over the output file size the user saw. Scale this to an enterprise generating longer, higher resolution video and the back-end footprint grows into the hundreds of gigabytes per clip. And that back-end footprint is growing fast. McKinsey projects AI inference demand will grow at a 35% CAGR through 2030, versus 22% for training, making up more than 40% of total data center demand by the end of the decade. A recent WD-sponsored IDC global white paper that surveyed 763 IT and business decision makers found that nearly 95% of respondents reported an increase in data volume due to generative AI over the past 12 months, with roughly 61% reporting growth of 25% or more. “You can think of having more storage as an optimization for keeping the GPUs busy,” said a senior product manager of a hyperscale cloud service provider quoted in report. “The biggest challenge is to ensure that the GPUs are not idle, waiting for data to show up—hours of GPU idle time becomes very expensive, very fast.” Put in dollar terms: if your storage spend is growing 25% year over year while your compute budget grows in the more predictable cyclical way, storage ends up outpacing the budget line that receives the most attention. Compute demand rises and falls with training cycles, product launches, and hardware refreshes. But the volume of retained data doesn't fall back down with it when compute demand slows. Model logs, synthetic outputs, inference results, and more keep accumulating, and organizations are holding onto more of it for longer. This changes the role of retained data. Information that once moved into an archive and rarely surfaced can now become an input to future AI workloads. As more data is retained, retrieved, and reused, storage must support not only growing capacity, but increasingly dynamic access to it. The same WD-IDC survey found 96% of respondents anticipate needing faster archive retrieval to support inference or retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). AI workloads require both performance and capacity, with different technologies serving different needs across the data lifecycle. And for the vast volumes of data that must be retained and revisited, HDDs provide a cost-effective, high-capacity foundation, with a significant cost-per-terabyte advantage For a CFO or CIO, this means “AI infrastructure spend” may be scoped too narrowly on compute. A model that idles while data loads costs about as much as one idling for lack of a GPU. But the storage bottleneck stays hidden until it’s already a problem, surfacing in operating costs and retrieval delays rather than a CapEx line next to the accelerators. Consider a retailer generating AI product videos for its catalog, or an insurer running claims documents through a RAG pipeline for faster underwriting. In both cases, the compute cost is a known, budgeted quantity: a per-generation or per-query rate that finance can forecast. The storage cost is different: It compounds with every video generated and every claim processed, because none of that back-end data disappears once the task is finished. A team that budgets AI as “compute plus a rounding error for storage” may find, in 12 to 18 months, that the rounding error has become a significant fraction of the program’s total cost—right around the time leadership is deciding whether to expand. Addressing this challenge starts with architecture. Storage strategy tends to be designed for expensive, hot-tier performance (for data accessed instantly and constantly), while most AI data by volume accumulates in the warm, cool, and cold tiers behind it. What enterprises need is economically scalable capacity: Infrastructure designed for the full AI data life cycle, balancing performance with capacity, power, reliability, and total cost of ownership as data persists, compounds, and is increasingly reused. That means treating archives that once sat untouched as active assets for RAG and inference, and setting retention policies for data’s growing strategic value rather than its original storage cost. It also means recognizing that decisions about storage play out across fleets, not individual drives. Such infrastructure requires storage tiers, where individualized power consumption, reliability, density, and operational efficiency all determine whether the system remains economically sustainable as capacity scales. There are three questions to ask about your organization’s AI infrastructure budget: Most organizations cannot answer all three with confidence. That’s the gap worth closing before the next budget cycle, not after. This content was produced by WD. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. We can do impossible things
2026-10-21

We can do impossible things

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-21

Visit Original Website
I’d wager everyone has a rehearsed response to what’s absolutely one of the world’s worst interview questions: “What’s your biggest weakness?” Whenever I’ve gotten it, I take a slow—even pensive—breath and rattle off my line: “I say no to myself too much.” I won’t lie. It’s a bit shameless—a definite humblebrag. I’m a systems thinker: When an idea pops up, I immediately spin up a feasibility assessment in my head. I run lists, plot timelines, and tally budget line items. Sometimes a rough Gantt chart starts to take shape. (I’m a gas at parties, as you can surely tell.) My brain, throughout my 20-plus-year career as a science and tech journalist, has played host to a near ­constant tug-of-war between ambition and reality. Learning to find that balance is a big part of what’s helped me survive in this industry and, ultimately, led me here. This past spring, I had the great privilege of joining the incredible team of journalists behind MIT Technology Review to take the reins of the print magazine. If you’ve been reading along with us for the last handful of issues, you may have noticed a few changes starting to materialize. We’ve brought back a regular roster of lists and infographics (both things that magazines do particularly well). But the biggest change is only just beginning to take shape, which is why Mat Honan, our editor in chief, asked me to hop in to introduce myself—and the issue you’re holding in your hands right now. We’re giving ourselves permission to play around and experiment. To be a little … audacious. Because, practical though I may be, I do still have plenty of ideas. It’s just that I sometimes need a reminder to stop blocking myself and do the thing. That’s the spirit driving this issue in particular. We wanted to end 2026 with a slate of stories that check in on impossible ideas. Why? Because it’s frankly a lot of fun to dream about living on the moon or uploading our brains. Because losing sight of ambition is all too easy when it feels as if we’re playing whack-a-mole with the world’s problems. But also because the scientists and engineers who absolutely refuse to give up on even the most daring endeavors—like making synthetic blood or decoding animals’ speech—remind us what this whole technology thing is supposed to do to begin with. It’s supposed to take cutting-edge research and development and apply it to make life better, safer, healthier, even more joyful. And when we eff up and make things worse (see: forever chemicals, traffic deaths, and AI that encourages potentially dangerous user behavior), we’re supposed to dig back in, assess the damage, and find ways to fix what we’ve broken. Even when unraveling the mess might itself seem impossible. Right before I came to MIT Technology Review, I edited a newsletter about individual actions that can help address the climate crisis, which some would call—and have called—a fool’s errand, given the sheer scale of the problem. They might say it’s as implausible as an AI kill switch, practical nuclear fusion, or a superconductor that works at room temperature. But if I learned nothing else from three years spent finding ways for li’l old me (or li’l old you!) to chip away at one of humanity’s biggest existential threats, it’s that focusing on what we can’t do only makes the darkness darker. So yeah, this one’s for the folks who have the audacity to try, and who refuse to let anyone—themselves included—tell them no. A pastor and his wife created a cryptocurrency and hawked it in Christian communities. When it came crashing down, investors lost millions―and they were accused of fraud. And the chances of a crackdown are slim, as the authorities spy opportunities for surveillance. Trevor Wischnewsky is making sure that the sky stays organized as we enter the age of food delivery by drone. A story about self-aware cities, direct democracy, and a steamy romance. 1Password lets AI agents sign in for users without seeing their passwords
2026-10-22

1Password lets AI agents sign in for users without seeing their passwords

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
An illustration of 1Password asking a user to approve an AI agent’s login. 1Password has launched 1Password for Agents, a platform that lets personal AI agents sign in to accounts on a person’s behalf without ever seeing the password. Agents from Meta, OpenAI, Anthropic and SpaceXSI are among the first to plug in, the Toronto-based company said on Thursday. Agents now book holidays, pay recurring bills and prepare expense reports. To do that, they often need access to the accounts behind those tasks. Many people simply paste a password into the chat, which leaves a copy somewhere they no longer control, 1Password said. The new system works in a few steps. When an agent reaches a login page, it proves its identity to 1Password with its own digital key. The person then sees which login the agent wants and why, and approves or rejects the request on their own device. If they approve, 1Password types the details straight into the login form rather than passing them to the agent. The password stays in the person’s vault. “An agent should be able to do the job it’s been asked to do without getting permanent access to everything behind the account,” said Aashish Tripathi, 1Password’s chief product officer. The launch partners include Muse, the personal assistant from Meta Superintelligence Labs, which asks 1Password for logins as it works through a task. OpenAI’s ChatGPT Work and dots, Anthropic’s Claude Duet, SpaceXSI’s Grok Bot and Town’s Townie assistant are also on the list. Claude Duet’s sign-ins will follow soon, the company said. “I’ve been using 1Password since 2007 and I’m so happy to have it integrated directly into Muse,” said Nat Friedman, head of product at Meta Superintelligence Labs. Browserbase, Browser Use, Perplexity, Ramp and five other companies are also signed up, with more to come. 1Password gave no pricing or launch dates for individual partners. The platform builds on Secure Agentic Autofill, an early-access feature 1Password released with Browserbase in October 2025. A researcher recently described how one hacker used AI agents to break into more than 100 websites. 1Password said its business vault protects more than 1.6 billion credentials and secrets for over 200,000 businesses.
