Xona raises $250M at a $2.45B valuation to build a rival to GPS
2026-10-22

Xona raises $250M at a $2.45B valuation to build a rival to GPS

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Xona, a California startup building a satellite navigation network in low Earth orbit, has raised $250 million at a $2.45 billion valuation. Steve Jurvetson’s Future Ventures led the Series D, Xona said on Thursday. The round comes seven months after a $170 million Series C. It takes Xona’s total funding since 2019 past $570 million. Future Ventures put in $78.5 million, Forbes reported. “We started the year with technically an empty warehouse, and now we have a functional satellite factory,” Brian Manning, Xona’s co-founder and chief executive, told Forbes. Manning, a former SpaceX engineer, said the company had not planned to raise again so soon. It did so after a surge in demand, he said. Xona’s network, called Pulsar, is built to run alongside GPS, which the US government operates. The company says Pulsar’s signals are up to 100 times more powerful than GPS and accurate to the centimetre. It says they resist jamming and can reach receivers indoors. Billions of devices rely on signals that can be jammed or spoofed, Xona said. Updating government systems is slow and costly, it added. Its test satellite, Pulsar-0, went up last year. Since then Xona has run more than 500 transmissions and shown accuracy better than two centimetres, Manning wrote in a company blog post. He said existing commercial receivers can pick up the signal without hardware changes. In August, the Federal Communications Commission authorised Xona’s constellation. The company calls it the first and only such licence for a private navigation network operating alongside government systems. This month Xona launched six Pulsar satellites, its largest launch so far. Four will support beta service in several countries from early 2027. Xona designed and built the other two entirely in-house, at its factory in Burlingame, California. They will test the design for the full constellation, which Manning said is meant for production at several satellites a week. “In just over a year, the company has advanced from a validation mission to deploying the foundation of an entirely new global navigation network,” said Steve Jurvetson, co-founder of Future Ventures. Jurvetson, a longtime SpaceX board member, has backed only three space companies: SpaceX, Planet and Xona. They map onto the three main satellite markets, Forbes noted: communications, imaging the Earth and navigation. Xona is not alone in scaling up in low Earth orbit. Amazon built its 1,000th Leo satellite this month for its broadband network. In Europe, Open Cosmos raised €300 million in September to scale satellite manufacturing. And this week, SpaceX’s quarterly results showed its AI business overtaking Starlink. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Microsoft says China-aligned hackers used AI agents in ConnectWise attack
2026-10-22

Microsoft says China-aligned hackers used AI agents in ConnectWise attack

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Microsoft says a China-aligned hacking group used AI agents to breach ConnectWise’s Cloud Backup service, opening a route into customers’ Microsoft 365 accounts. Microsoft Threat Intelligence published its findings on Storm-0940 on October 21. ConnectWise disclosed the breach in a security advisory on October 16, the same day security firm Huntress published its initial account of the attack. ConnectWise Cloud Backup, previously known as SkyKick, allows IT service providers to back up clients’ Microsoft 365 data. The service relies on applications with broad access to customer accounts, making those app identities a potential route into connected systems. According to Microsoft, Storm-0940 compromised several of those identities and used them to access customer accounts. In some cases, the attackers added new authentication keys and changed administrator roles to maintain access. The group has been active since 2021 and has targeted organisations across multiple sectors, including defence, government, law, energy and semiconductor manufacturing, Microsoft said. For this campaign, the attackers used ARTEX, a free, open-source hacking tool built around AI agents that can run on different AI models. Such tools can also be used by security teams to test their own systems. Microsoft said the agents scanned networks, attempted to guess passwords, and exploited application permissions with limited human involvement. It did not identify the AI model used in the attack. “Agents are not constrained by factors affecting humans,” Microsoft’s researchers wrote. Storm-0940 attempted to sign in to more than 2,250 Microsoft 365 tenants, with activity reaching 2,900 attempts per minute. Microsoft said the group gained high-level cloud access in some cases within three hours, although most attempts failed. Huntress, which monitors customer systems, had confirmed breaches at 162 tenants by October 16. It later identified nearly 1,000 tenants that had received sign-in attempts from the attackers, with more than 300 showing successful attempts. ConnectWise disabled seven application identities on October 17 and took Cloud Backup offline. The company has not publicly responded to Microsoft’s attribution of the attack to Storm-0940. A ConnectWise spokesperson told The Record there was “no indication” that backup data had been compromised. Microsoft said the incident did not result from a vulnerability in its own systems. It advised customers to keep their ConnectWise integrations disabled until the company confirms they can be safely restored, and to review sign-in logs dating back to at least October 15. ConnectWise began restoring the service in stages on October 21, and the company expects recovery to take several days. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Google funds a 1GW virtual power plant with Voltus on the PJM grid
2026-10-22

Google funds a 1GW virtual power plant with Voltus on the PJM grid

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
Google will fund a 1 gigawatt virtual power plant run by Voltus on the PJM grid, to supply capacity for its operations there. The deal adds 1,070 megawatts to the 100 megawatts the two agreed in June, the companies said on Thursday. PJM serves 67 million customers across 13 states and the District of Columbia. The companies called the new capacity one of the largest known distributed portfolios a hyperscaler has deployed. A virtual power plant has no turbines. Voltus pools spare capacity from local businesses and homes, and Google pays for it. When the grid is under strain, Voltus signals those sites to cut their power use. They do it with batteries, smart thermostats, changes to industrial processes and other flexible equipment. Voltus then passes the money on to the people taking part, through bill savings and direct payments. The scheme runs under its Bring Your Own Capacity programme. The companies cited a recent report from The Brattle Group. It predicts US consumers could save more than $100bn over the next decade by using the grid better. “Expanding our initial commitment tenfold demonstrates our core effort to actively bring our own capacity to the grids that serve us,” said Ellen Zuckerman, Google’s head of energy market development for the Americas. Buying the capacity this way means Google’s operations in PJM support the region’s reliability, she said. It also brings money to local communities. It is Google’s second big power deal this month. It signed a 20-year agreement with Constellation for 890MW of nuclear power on 6 October. In September, Google and Nvidia launched an alliance to make data centres flex their power use. Voltus raised a $225 million Series D on 8 October. It connects batteries, on-site generation, flexible load and electric vehicles to electricity markets. “This significant expansion of our work with Google translates data center growth into direct economic benefits for businesses and homes,” said Dana Guernsey, Voltus’s chief executive. eMudhra drops TLS certificates days after browsers blocked its keys
2026-10-22

eMudhra drops TLS certificates days after browsers blocked its keys

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
eMudhra is leaving the global SSL/TLS certificate business, days after browsers blocked eight of its certificates. The Indian certificate authority’s board decided to withdraw voluntarily, the company said on Thursday. The business brings in about 1% of eMudhra’s global revenue, its chairman, V. Srinivasan, said. Data sovereignty and compliance rules have made it more complex and costly to run, he said. Some large players also issue the certificates for free, which has cut its potential and return for eMudhra. “However, the level of social media amplification around this incident has brought scrutiny that is out of proportion to SSL/TLS’s place in our business,” the company said. The incident was the discovery that the private keys of eight eMudhra intermediate certificates were weak enough to calculate. One was the number 4. Chrome, Firefox, Apple and Microsoft have blocked them. Srinivasan said eMudhra revoked the affected certificate authorities within 14 hours of discovery and disclosed the problem promptly. The affected authorities issued no certificates to websites or users, he said. Nobody breached its infrastructure or data, he added. In a statement on Wednesday, eMudhra blamed a flaw in a key generation ceremony held on 30 April 2025. A wrap and unwrap step produced a second set of weak keys alongside the strong ones, it said, and those weak keys went into browser root stores. The certificate authorities had passed independent audits two years in a row, the company said. An independent researcher worked out the keys, possibly with AI-assisted techniques, it added. India’s CERT-In said last week that AI agents under testing had broken into live systems at Indian firms. eMudhra’s G1 RSA roots will keep serving customers until the move is complete, it said. It is working with the browser programmes and will contact affected customers to replace their certificates free of charge through partners. Its revocation and certificate status services will run until every certificate it issued has expired or a replacement is in place. eMudhra said it will also complete its remaining commitments to the browser root programmes, including a full root-cause report. “For many certifying authorities, SSL/TLS is the whole business. For eMudhra it is one part of a much broader digital solutions portfolio,” the company said. The withdrawal leaves the rest of the business untouched, eMudhra said. That includes its digital signature certificates and eSign services in India and elsewhere, its emCA and CertiNext enterprise PKI platforms, the emSigner eSignature suite, and the SecurePass and emAS identity products. The company trades in Mumbai and works in more than 35 countries. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Millions of PHP2Shell attempts so far, but officials see no break-in
2026-10-22

Millions of PHP2Shell attempts so far, but officials see no break-in

The Next Web | 2026-10-22

Visit Original Website
PHP runs on web servers behind a large share of the world’s websites Hackers have made millions of attempts to exploit PHP2Shell since Wednesday’s disclosure, Cloudflare’s chief executive, Matthew Prince, said on X. Officials in Germany, the Netherlands and Japan say they have not seen one succeed. Some stats on #php2shell. In the 10 hours before it was publicly announced, across the @Cloudflare network we saw thousands of attempts to exploit it. That jumped to tens of thousands in the 45 minutes post announcement, hundreds of thousands within 2 hours, and millions now. — Matthew Prince 🌥 (@eastdakota) October 22, 2026 Cloudflare saw thousands of attempts in the 10 hours before the announcement. That jumped to tens of thousands in the next 45 minutes and hundreds of thousands within two hours, Prince wrote early on Thursday. Germany’s cyber agency, the BSI, has seen attacks but no successful use of the flaws, its vice-president, Thomas Caspers, told Politico. “It seems a little bit more complicated than expected to exploit. When we had the planning process before the release of the patch, we were expecting a much faster track of the attackers,” Caspers said. The head of the Dutch cyber agency, Matthijs van Amelsfort, told Politico it had no indication the flaw was being actively misused. The US cyber agency CISA said in its alert on Wednesday it had not seen exploitation at the time. Elizabeth Barron, executive director of the PHP Foundation, told CNBC on Wednesday that it had seen no working exploits. Matt Suiche, a researcher at the security startup Tolmo, told CNBC that his team had built one in less than a day. “At the moment it doesn’t look like exploitation has taken off or been widespread,” Ryan Austin, the Calif researcher who helped find the flaws, told the Guardian. The disruption spread beyond websites across Europe. Japan’s prime minister, Sanae Takaichi, urged site owners on X to install the fix as soon as it came out. “We have an urgent request for everyone to take action tonight,” Takaichi wrote, in a translation by Business Insider. Mizuho’s bank and securities arms halted services on Thursday, Bloomberg reported. Shizuoka Bank suspended its website and app, and companies in the MUFG group halted some online services. All Nippon Airways disabled boarding passes in Apple Wallet, saying it had found no evidence of a breach. Japan Airlines, East Japan Railway and Seven-Eleven Japan took some web services down. Chief Cabinet Secretary Minoru Kihara said no attacks exploiting the flaws had been confirmed. In France, Orange suspended access to some websites and apps while it deployed the patches. In Britain, a government spokesperson said it was working to identify any exposure in its own online services, the BBC reported. South Korea raised its cyber alert level for the private sector, the Guardian said. The attempts started before the flaws were public. Someone tried to exploit PHP2Shell on 17 October, four days before disclosure, Amazon said. The attempt hit its decoy servers from an anonymised address. CJ Moses, Amazon’s chief information security officer, said it was unclear how the attacker got exploit details during the embargo. OpenAI, which led the coalition behind the emergency patches, had said that, to its knowledge, AI found the flaws ahead of attackers. Amazon logged more attempts from several actors in the hours before the 14:00 UTC release. “PHP2Shell is now being broadly exploited in the wild,” Moses wrote. Analysis was still going on, he said. It was unclear whether any attempt would have worked on real systems. Amazon gave no information on any victims, Bloomberg reported. The disclosure itself is now in dispute. The PHP Foundation has said Chainguard broke the embargo by publishing patched images more than six hours before the release. Chainguard’s chief executive, Dan Lorenc, told The Register his company did not leak. He said it shipped the fixes because people were already being hit, and would not do anything differently. “It’s coordinated vulnerability disclosure, unless there’s evidence of active exploits in the wild,” Lorenc said.
